#238. Nike lays out financial goals ahead of first investor day in 9 years

Nike said Tuesday ahead of its first investor day in nine years that it was reaffirming its 2027 financial guidance.

The retailer also laid out a financial framework and said that by the end of fiscal 2031 it's targeting approximately $5 billion in incremental revenue growth from fiscal year 2027, operating margins of 12% to 14% and adjusted earnings per share of $3.00 to $3.50. The company said higher-quality revenue, gross margin expansion and productivity improvements would help it reach its goals.

Nike's 2027 financial guidance includes expectations for revenue to decline in the high-single-digit range, an adjusted operating margin of approximately 6%, and adjusted EPS of $1.15 to $1.35.

The company also introduced a strategy it's calling Full Stride Ahead, which the company said aims "to get closer to athletes and focus resources on the greatest opportunities for long-term growth."

Nike CEO Elliott Hill told CNBC's Sara Eisen that the new initiative is about "being closer to the athletes we serve."

"What's different today is the depth and breadth we're taking from an innovation perspective," Hill said.

He added that Nike is emphasizing sportswear at a time when sports have never been more relevant around the world. Hill also said Nike is working to regain its cultural relevance, after a perceived dropoff contributed to its slowdown both in its home market and in China.

Nike said it would focus on performance sports and sportswear opportunities "with the greatest potential for long-term growth," and said its five priority sports would be running, training, global football, basketball and outdoor.

Nike is holding an investor day at its Oregon headquarters Tuesday as the company aims to prove to investors that its turnaround strategy is yielding success after a yearslong slump.

Last month, the company reported mixed fiscal first-quarter results as it announced a restructuring plan, including layoffs that it said would start next year. It's the company's third round of layoffs to be announced this year.

Nike's net income and revenue both fell during the most recent period compared with a year ago, and the company said it expects revenue to decline by a high single-digit percentage in fiscal 2027.

The new strategy, dubbed Pace, focuses on the company's supply chain and reorganizing into three geographies. Nike said the turnaround plan is expected to deliver roughly $2.5 billion in savings through fiscal 2031.

Nike has been seeing significant sustained declines in its China market, where revenue last quarter dropped 26%. Experts have said the company is losing relevance in the country, while Nike has said it's actively working to win over younger consumers.

At the same time, Nike announced last quarter that it's creating a new global campus in India as part of its new growth strategy, hoping the country will help boost its performance. The company said it was remaining focused on China even as it begins to build out its India presence.

Nike COO Venkatesh Alagirisamy told CNBC the company sees an opportunity to triple its business in the country over the next five years.

He added that Nike's broader strategy will position it for success in India.

"If you look at the three components, brand building, landing those innovations into marketplaces in an increasingly locally connected fashion and then doing all of those things in a much faster pace really aligns well with the aspirations for India," he said.

CEO Elliott Hill's turnaround strategy includes improving different parts of the business at different rates based on priority. While it's seen some signs of progress in the North America business, Hill has said that performance isn't enough to offset the pressures on the company elsewhere, including internationally.

The Nike consumer has also been under increased macroeconomic pressure as geopolitical tensions mount and inflation rises.

Shares of Nike have fallen 50% over the past two years.

#237. My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

My hometown shows that high streets have to change or die

The empty building that used to be the home of Marks & Spencer in Aberdeen is one of the first things you see when you arrive off the train and come into the city centre. The sign has gone, though the shape of the lettering can still be made out; the windows are blacked out and the doors boarded up.

This is my hometown. As a teenager, I spent hours on Union Street, its main thoroughfare, nicknamed the Granite Mile after the local silver-grey stone. It was the place to try on all the latest fashions from the likes of Clockhouse and Chelsea Girl.

The street used to be packed with shoppers from across the north-east of Scotland.

"I come to Union Street to take a bus somewhere else... there's nothing to shop for nowâ¦ it's horrendous," complains Marie Sim, a lifelong Aberdeen resident.

It's a similar story across the country: High Streets, once bursting with shops, hit first by the rise of out-of-town retail centres and then by online commerce.

But Aberdeen is in the thick of an ambitious turnaround plan. The port city has essentially declared war on its empty shop problem, taking an unusually hands-on approach to filling vacant units and bringing long-neglected upper floors of buildings into use.

The Marks & Spencer flagship store in Aberdeen closed in August, 80 years after it opened there

On top of that, the council is pouring tens of millions into trying to make the city centre a destination again. Like many other places, it's leaning into the "experience economy", with a new indoor food and drink market and a major redevelopment of the seafront.

High Streets are integral to how we feel about where we live. The sight of vacant shops and graffiti can quickly become a vicious circle, deterring shoppers, discouraging investment and leading to anti-social behaviour.

Politicians of all stripes have been trying for years to breathe new life into town centres - and Andy Burnham, who has made a focus on "place" part of his political identity, is the latest. He argues they should become "symbols of Britain's renaissance".

But can it be achieved? And if they're to survive, do town centres need a fundamental rethink?

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant, according to Green Street, the property and retail analytics company, a number that's stayed stubbornly high for years.

"If there was a silver bullet, it would have been fired years ago," says Jackie Sadek, a former government adviser on urban regeneration, who has spent nearly four decades working in town centre renewal.

One of the biggest obstacles is fractured ownership. Shops and buildings are often owned by dozens of different landlords, sometimes through offshore companies or complex structures, each with their own interests and priorities. That can slow decision-making and make large-scale regeneration difficult.

But even with empty units that are available, you need to find someone willing to take them on.

One in eight properties on British High Streets are currently vacant

The cost of running a High Street business has also risen; employment costs are higher, and there's the burden of business rates.

Firms need customers. Footfall on many High Streets has been in long-term decline as shopping and services move online, and this enormous structural shift has yet to play out fully.

Two-thirds of town centre experts think there is still 20% to 40% too much retail space, according to a survey by retail body Revo and real estate consultancy Lambert Smith Hampton.

In Aberdeen's boom years of North Sea oil, from the late 1970s through to the early 2010s, it didn't have to work hard to fill its hotels and restaurants and get the cash tills ringing because of the wealth the energy industry brought to the city.

I remember weekends with friends as a teenager, spending whatever cash we had in the fashion chains and shoe shops. Later came the pubs and bar-hopping, and trips to the art deco Capitol Theatre (now office space), where I saw Duran Duran.

But by the end of 2022, Union Street had hit rock bottom. Aberdonians had long deserted the High Street for a shopping centre, which opened in 2009 next to the railway station.

"They'd fallen out of love with their city," says Bob Keiller, head of Our Union Street coalition, a community-led group formed after an emergency summit to reverse the decline.

The first thing it did was listen to what residents wanted, receiving thousands of submissions. Restoring a bit of civic pride and filling empty units became the focus.

It's been a co-ordinated effort, involving letting agents, the council, landlords, businesses and a big team of volunteers, led by Keiller, a former FTSE 100 CEO, who isn't getting paid. He has a map on his office wall, detailing the status of every property, unit by unit, with colour-coded Post-it notes.

They audited the street, tracking down absent landlords, and logging what was empty and why. Volunteers offered to give the empty properties a clean and a lick of paint, to make them more attractive to let. They also spruced up the streets, picking litter, weeding, and painting bins. A small team still goes out once a fortnight.

Bob Keiller heads a community-led group set up to help revive Aberdeen's Union Street

There's a website where all the available properties are collated; crucially, all of the financial incentives are also listed. These can include council fit-out grants (where a new business gets money to decorate premises), and a two-year discount from business rates.

In 2023, 57 of the 198 units on Union Street were empty. As of today, 33 have been filled, with a further 14 in the process of being let.

But it can feel like a game of whack-a-mole because since the initiative started, another 20 units have become vacant. "The key thing is, we're filling quicker than emptying," Keiller insists.

Keiller's operations director, David McKeith, describes it as putting an arm round the shoulder of those they can help and being a "wasp in the ear" of people who just say no.

At one end of the office, a small, young team produces social media content, linking visitors and residents to a new Our Aberdeen app, showing what the city can offer.

For Keiller, the "small wins" - like tackling graffiti - are needed to help change public perception.

"We've realised the psychology is not to do it once but be prepared to do it two or three times before people that do these things eventually say, 'there's no point in putting it there because somebody's going to paint over it.'"

Attention is also turning to the often-forgotten upper floors of buildings along the once-iconic Union Street. In a separate initiative run by the business group Aberdeen Inspired, experts are getting inside those vacant high-up spaces to identify the barriers to bringing them back into use.

Aberdeen sought the help of a High Street troubleshooter, Iain Nicholson, who's worked with dozens of towns and cities through his Vacant Shops Academy.

Out of more than 1,000 empty units he helped audit nationally, on average more than half didn't have a letting agent actively marketing the property. He says some councils don't even know who the landlords are.

Empty shops, he says, won't all be filled by the market alone. Towns and cities need to encourage a broader range of occupiers, from arts and culture to leisure, health and well-being.

He says what's striking about Aberdeen is its joined-up approach, and the fact people "rolled up their sleeves" and overcame differences. "That's not something you can yet say about everywhere."

Every town and city faces its own mix of challenges.

Nicholson also helped Crewe in Cheshire. As a smaller place than Aberdeen, it is focusing on arts, culture, and community, with a new women's centre opened this month.

And English councils are being encouraged to take a more interventionist role.

They can now force an empty commercial unit that's been vacant for at least a year to be offered to tenants through a High Street Rental Auction, even if the landlord doesn't want to let it.

A noodle bar (left) is one of the new businesses in the area. Parts of the city centre have been undergoing construction work (right)

It's early days but the mere prospect of losing control of a property is delivering some results. Bassetlaw Council in Nottinghamshire, an early adopter of the scheme, says more than 30 shops across the district have been let or are currently under offer as a result of the policy.

But filling empty units is only part of the challenge. The bigger question is what town and city centres are actually for.

Changing habits mean we won't need so many shops. Instead, it's argued, town centres will need more people living and working in them, with new homes and office space alongside services that can't be done online. With an ageing population, for example, bringing NHS health centres into the heart of a town could be a big opportunity.

In Aberdeen, the building that used to house BHS - a department store shuttered in 2016 - is being converted into an entrance plaza for the new food and drink market.

The physical appearance of Union Street is changing too. Like many Victorian shopping thoroughfares, it was built with a wide road (probably to give space to horses and carriages). Now parts of the pavements are being widened to make it a more pleasant place to spend time, though many Aberdonians are weary of the disruption, and of new traffic restrictions.

Money is a big barrier. Aberdeen's new indoor food and drink market is costing Â£40m, funded by the council and the UK Levelling Up Fund. In other places, regeneration schemes can struggle because the cost of redevelopment outweighs the likely financial return, making support from the public sector essential. And the economics are becoming more challenging.

"Higher interest rates, rising costs of construction and other issues are impacting the viability and delivery of new housing and big redevelopment projects," says Steve Norris, a director at Lambert Smith Hampton. He says that big projects usually require cash from both the public and private sectors.

He wants to see "more creative ways of funding".

Part of Burnham's plan is to give local leaders more freedom to invest in town centres and drive regeneration, building on the approach he has championed in Greater Manchester. But bigger projects take years.

Volunteers have played a role in the redevelopment of Aberdeen's Union Street

Making it easier and cheaper to open and run a High Street business is one of the more immediate solutions being called for, with business rates a particular concern.

Burnham has promised further rates reform. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go. All eyes are on this month's Budget to see how far he will go in England. Business rates are devolved in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland.

The chief executive of Next, Simon Wolfson, argues that the bigger challenge is not business rates, but planning reform.

"The most important thing that the government can do for high streets is let them develop," says Wolfson, who's also a Conservative peer.

Back in Aberdeen, there are some signs of traction.

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street in 2024. A council grant helped with the refurbishment after they decided to make the leap.

Can they make the sums add up? "We're getting there," she says.

She feels the same about the future of her street. "I always say Rome wasn't built in a day."

Lauren Reid and her mother Rose moved their fashion boutique to Union Street with the help of a council grant

Right now the area feels like a construction site - but another gaping hole will soon be filled. Robert Keane is a local property developer who in 2023 bought the Trinity shopping centre, once home to Debenhams. The space is now set to be re-occupied by a national leisure brand. A notoriously tiring stairway leading to the Trinity from the train station is also being replaced by an escalator.

"We saw a real good opportunity, because it was dying," he says.

He reckons he can make a go of it, cutting the cost of parking and overheads, resetting rents and being more nimble than previous far-flung owners.

It was a bold move given Lambert Smith Hampton suggests that more than one in 10 UK shopping centres is ripe for demolition.

But he's betting the dynamics of Aberdeen are finally changing.

"I think by 2027 it should look and feel like a different city."

That is certainly the hope. Yet walking along Union Street today, past the boarded-up shopfronts and roadworks, it can still feel like a city caught between its past and whatever comes next.

#235. Trump, Xi, Putin said they plan to boost oil production, revamp UN after trilateral talks

The U.S., China and Russia on Thursday vowed to boost production of petroleum products and reaffirmed their commitment to reducing nuclear risks following their leaders' historic trilateral summit.

President Donald Trump, Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin "had an in-depth exchange of views on the current international situation and committed to maintaining strategic stability and international security," the countries said in identical statements shared after the meeting in Shenzhen, China.

The statements were brief and broad, containing few details about what the leaders agreed on during their talks. They did not include any mention of Russia's war against Ukraine, which continues to rage in eastern Europe despite recent U.S. efforts to broker a ceasefire. They also made no reference to the U.S.' war in Iran, in which Russia and China have both been accused of aiding Tehran.

"The three countries plan to increase the production and supply of refined petroleum products for the benefit of all nations. They reaffirmed the importance of stable energy flows," according to the statements, described as a "Heads of State Declaration toward Peace."

The three presidents also "reaffirmed that a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought," and committed to a "dialogue on reducing nuclear risks and preventing the proliferation of nuclear weapons."

And they committed to "reform" the United Nations so it "can respond more effectively to global challenges and conform to its fundamental mission of international peace and security," according to the statements.

Trump holds talks separately with Putin and Xi ahead of their historic trilateral meeting

Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

The leaders also agreed that "the International Criminal Court and other similar bodies must not abuse their jurisdiction," the declarations stated. The Trump administration has heavily criticized the ICC, and Secretary of State Marco Rubio last month announced new U.S. sanctions against what he called a "rogue court."

In opening remarks earlier Thursday, Trump said the U.S., Russia and China are "uniquely positioned" to reduce the world's nuclear weapons stockpile.

"As the world's three largest nuclear powers by far, the United States, Russia and China are uniquely positioned to discuss how to reduce the number of nuclear weapons on earth and also to lessen the risk that those weapons will ever be used," Trump said.

"The power is unbelievable, unthinkable," he said, adding that it's "a very hard thing" for countries to reduce nuclear weapons.

That would include not letting Iran have nuclear weapons, Trump said.

He said the three leaders would also discuss the war between Russia and Ukraine, and criticized "rogue globalist organizations," including the International Criminal Court.

The closely watched meeting lasted about an hour and a half late Thursday afternoon China time, following a working lunch between Trump and Xi that started at least an hour later than originally planned.

Trump told Xi and Putin they were "really making history" with the first-ever trilateral meeting between the three countries' leaders.

He said it was an "honor" to be at the meeting with Putin and Xi, whom he congratulated on a "very successful APEC summit." The three were among the world leaders attending the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

"Too often past leaders have avoided talking to nations with whom they had disagreements," Trump added. "We feel the opposite."

At the trilateral, Xi said that for large nations, having good interactions is not a choice but a necessity, according to a translation by state media.

In the report, Xi noted China's partnership with Russia, work towards constructive strategic stability in U.S. relations, and called on the three countries to respond to the world's challenges.

Xi laid out four "correct" ways for large countries to interact: mutual respect, peaceful coexistence, win-win cooperation, and adherence to dialogue, according to state media.

The Chinese president echoed Trump's comments on nuclear weapons, calling for such arms to be comprehensively prohibited and destroyed, adding that a nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought.

Putin said Russia, China and the U.S. can strengthen global stability if they cooperate constructively, calling for the three leaders to discuss energy security and global and regional issues — including the war in Ukraine.

Beijing only confirmed the closely watched meeting on Monday. The three leaders had briefly gathered Wednesday evening as part of a welcome banquet for the APEC leaders more broadly.

#232. Cathedral's staff review 'difficult but necessary'

Gloucester Cathedral will increasingly use events like concerts to fund the buildings maintenance

There will be no compulsory redundancies at a cathedral despite a restructure of staff, leaders have said.

Gloucester Cathedral, which costs about Â£10,500 a day to open, has been undergoing an organisational restructure due to rising costs as some staff have opted for voluntary redundancies.

It comes after cathedral bosses across the West of England said they had also been feeling the pinch, forcing them to find creative ways to raise money.

Dean of Gloucester Reverend Andrew Zihni said the decision to reshape the cathedral's money was "difficult but necessary" to ensure its future.

Could you live under deep-sea pressure for 28 days?

How to reduce fire risks at home this Christmas

Reduced street cleaning due to depot fire

Zihni said that visitors to the cathedral are unlikely to notice any changes.

Cathedral chiefs said the restructure involved a review of staff roles and responsibilities, as well as how the organisation is run.

A small number of staff had been made voluntarily redundant as a result of the changes.

In a statement, cathedral leaders said that these had been "very difficult decisions," but that these changes will ensure it will continue to serve the "city, county and wider community for generations to come".

Speaking to BBC Radio Gloucestershire, Zihni said the cathedral had a number of costs, including heating, maintenance and staffing, as well as safeguarding, education, worship, music and heritage.

"We have to raise the money year on year through donations, visitors, grants, fundraising and other income because we don't have any automatic funding either from the national church or the government," he said.

Gloucester Cathedral is not the only one in the country with high maintenance costs.

Anna Pitt, executive director of the Association of English Cathedrals, said it costs more than Â£30,000 a day to "simply open the doors" at Canterbury Cathedral and York Minster.

"The scale of what cathedrals offer is considerable. They employ 6,000 people across the sector, welcome nearly 10 million visitors each year and are open 365 days a year," said Pitt.

Reverend Andrew Zhini was made Dean of Gloucester Cathedral in 2023

Zihni said the Chapter, who are responsible for running the cathedral, are also looking at ways to "diversify their income stream and develop commercial angles".

He said: "People may have seen some of it already with the concerts we've been hosting in the cathedral, but there isn't a separation between the commercial activity and the regular activity," he said.

#226. Leftover pumpkins help deliver 1,000th free meal

Alex Vardill from Cambridge Sustainable Food says local growers have donated leftover pumpkins and squashes after Halloween

Leftover pumpkins have helped a sustainable food organisation provide its 1,000th free meal to low-income households.

Cambridge Sustainable Food started producing recipe kits earlier this year using fruit and vegetables that would have otherwise gone to waste.

The group received masses of pumpkins from local farmers after Halloween, including about 300kg (661lb) of them from one supplier alone.

Alex Vardill, from the organisation, said many of the meal kits had been received by families who sometimes struggled to access fresh produce.

"This has been a real help with improving that access at a time when food is really expensive," the 25-year-old said.

"They provide everything you need to make a meal in one bag, already measured out, with a recipe there in front of you."

The kits are funded by Cambridge City Council and South Cambridgeshire District Council.

They are distributed by food hubs â a mini-supermarket where anyone can go to access low-cost food â and other local organisations.

Cambridge Sustainable Food launched its recipe kits in the summer

The Halloween leftovers were used in 50 pumpkin and lentil soup recipe kits, which were assembled by a team of volunteers this week.

The organisation is based in a warehouse in the Barnwell area of Cambridge, which is stocked with food donated by local supermarkets and residents.

Vardill, who also volunteers at a community kitchen, said they had noticed an increased demand for both services.

"It's a really tough time for a lot of people," they added.

The recipe kit initiative launched in July, making use of excess blackberries.

Since then, other recipes have been themed around leftover tomatoes, courgettes, and apples.

#223. Gold's bad year is getting worse. Why its price keeps falling

Precious metals investors can be forgiven if they feel like the past year has given them whiplash.

Following a torrid run, which saw the price of gold hit new records 53 times in 2025, gold hit a historic high of $5,589.38 an ounce on Jan. 28, only to take an almost equally historic nosedive. On Wednesday, gold fell below $3,800 for the first time since September 2025, roughly a third below its peak.

A convergence of factors pushed gold to historic highs earlier this year, according to Suki Cooper, global head of commodities research at Standard Chartered, the London-based multinational bank. Now a different convergence is bringing the price down.

"Gold can behave as a commodity or a currency," Cooper said.

Which role it plays depends on the factors dominating the market and the psyche of investors, and right now, it's the currency argument that is bringing the precious metal down.

Cooper said gold's climb began with strong physical demand that started in 2022 and raised the floor under prices. A second phase followed as investors rethought gold's place in their portfolios. With the cost of holding a non-yielding asset falling, they started thinking about the metal for the longer term. That, Cooper said, marked a turnaround.

Until early 2025, Cooper said, long-term investors, especially those in gold-backed exchange-traded funds, had been cutting their exposure, which effectively added supply to the market. Last year, that reversed: ETF holdings, led by the SPDR Gold Shares (GLD), rose by roughly 800 tons and helped absorb the market's excess supply.

"There was a lot of one-way interest, a lot of appetite to buy but not to sell," Cooper said.

With selling in the secondary market staying muted, prices kept rising. By the end of 2025, net ETF holdings had passed 4,000 tons. Globally, gold ETFs had their strongest year ever for inflows, $89 billion, led by North America, according to the World Gold Council, with assets more than doubling to $559 billion in total.

A weaker dollar, lower bond yields and worries about global debt "set the foundation for gold to hit highs in 2026," Cooper said.

Investors who had been under-exposed to the metal kept buying.

Evan Mills, a financial advisor with Scholar Financial Advising, a fee-only firm in Winston-Salem, N.C., said the lower interest rates, expectations of further rate cuts and a weakening dollar, were all reasons for investors to favor gold.

"It was a perfect spot to be in if you were an investor in gold and commodities as a whole," Mills said. And investors did extremely well, especially with lower interest rates. "Cash doesn't do well in that environment, so a lot of people looked toward other places to hold their cash and maintain purchasing power," Mills said.

The speed of the rally made some investors uneasy, and there was growing talk that the Fed could tighten. Then, Cooper noted, the Middle East conflict broke out and gold changed roles. It stopped acting as a portfolio safe haven and became "a liquidity provider."

Cooper explained that because it was liquid and easy to sell, investors sold it to raise cash, which set off the first correction. Since then, she said, markets have been pricing in what the Fed will do.

"In 2026, the Fed has leaned more toward higher interest rates," Mills said.

This week, the Federal Reserve Board of Governors member Christopher Waller said a 50-basis point rate hike may be necessary to "shock people."

"There are higher Treasury yields and a strengthening dollar, so the opportunity cost of holding an asset that doesn't produce income is much higher," Mills said.

Mills said that in the current situation, gold investors are missing out on an environment in which cash actually does well, with money market funds that can pay 4% to 5%.

"Cash maintains purchasing power, and you don't need to put it in an illiquid asset or one where you're relying on another person to see the value," Mills said.

From 2025 to 2026, gold didn't become any less scarce; the alternatives to owning it became more attractive.

"Stocks, bonds, the classic portfolio mix, all became a lot more attractive, so people looked at the opportunity cost and decided it wasn't worth holding gold anymore," Mills said.

This has been the case for other metals in 2026 as well, with silver plummeting from an all-time high price.

After an extraordinary rally, it's easy to start questioning gold. Mills said investors should start by remembering that expectations were extremely high. "When expectations are 20% or 30% returns in a year, then of course when returns drop, people start to panic," Mills said.

Panic shouldn't be the watchword for gold investors, but caution should remain.

"In the short term, with real yields going up and the dollar likely strengthening with future Fed hikes, I'd be cautious about gold in the overall portfolio," Mills said.

For investors, the key is to understand why gold is in your portfolio to begin with.

"If you're looking for it to be a short-term purchase, then gold likely doesn't belong in your portfolio at all," Mills said. If the metal is seen as a hedge, gold can remain a valuable part of a portfolio, but it can still be a poor investment depending on the price you pay to get in.

"If you're looking at gold to be the growth section of your portfolio, you're likely going to be disappointed," he said. "Gold is insurance, not an engine, and the mistake is buying insurance when its price has skyrocketed," he added.

For investors who bought at January's peak as a modest, long-term hedge, a sharp downturn doesn't defeat the purpose, he said. "You have nothing to really worry about, because that's what the whole goal of gold was."

Gold tends to do well when the dollar weakens or when markets and geopolitics turn uncertain, Mills said, and when those pressures fade, the rest of a portfolio should pick up the slack.

"That's the whole point of insurance, and the whole point of diversification," he said. "Gold does well when the other parts of your portfolio might be slacking, and when they flip-flop, that's a good thing."

Other commodities experts say gold remains a good long-term bet. Amy Gower, head of metals and mining strategy at Morgan Stanley, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" that there are "still lots of reasons" for investors to hang on to their gold despite the correction this year.

Philip Diehl, former director of the U.S. Mint and now president of U.S. Money Reserve, an Austin, Texas-based distributor of government-issued gold, silver and platinum coins, said the long-term case for gold is still in place, but the combination of abrupt shifts in U.S. trade policy and geopolitical alliances, along with the Iran war, qualifies as a black swan event for the precious metal.

"The fundamentals that drove that bull market remain in place. They were interrupted by the Cat 5 hurricane that was unleashed in the Persian Gulf in an unwise war," Diehl said.

He expects that to change after American voters "issued a mandate" to end that war two weeks ago.

"I'm confident gold prices will return to their upward trajectory in 2027 as oil prices normalize and prices fall at the pump, allowing interest rates to stabilize," Diehl said.

Central banks continue to buy gold, with the highest monthly total in September, according to the World Gold Council, though overall purchasing is 7% lower than the same period last year.

Marc Chandler, managing partner and chief market strategist at Bannockburn Capital Markets, said surveys suggest that, in aggregate, that will not change. "However, don't only think about China or U.S. rivals," Chandler said, noting that the World Gold Council has identified Poland, a NATO member, as one of the larger buyers for most of this year.

"Retail has been burned," Chandler said. "Higher interest rates have made non-interest-bearing assets less attractive, including bitcoin."

Chandler said that the price of gold is headed to $3,500 to $3,600. Gold hit a fresh low below $3,700 on Thursday morning.

"There is nothing on the charts that says a low is in place," Chandler said. "For the anxious bottom pickers, I urge caution and being patient to see some reversal pattern. For the most part, the decline looks fairly orderly."

"Gold is still very expensive compared to anything but the last two years," Chandler said, noting that gold's 10-year (120-month) moving average is about $2,127 and its 20-year (240-month) moving average is about $1,666.

#221. Increase in direct cremations since pandemic

SunLife's 2026 Cost of Dying Report found direct cremation was now chosen for around one in five funerals

Funeral directors say there has been an increase in direct cremations, with no mourners in attendance, since the Covid-19 pandemic.

Brian Ugalde, whose firm of funeral directors has worked in Cornwall for 135 years, said non-attendance had become more acceptable since the lockdowns when families were not always allowed to attend.

"A funeral is a ceremony... a farewell, a celebration... it is for for those left behind," he said, adding direct cremation came after Covid when "the model became acceptable and practical" as well as being cheaper.

SunLife's 2026 Cost of Dying Report found direct cremation was now chosen for about one in five funerals in the UK.

The report said, in the majority of cases, the deceased had requested or arranged a direct cremation themselves before their death.

In 86% of those cases, families decided to hold their own celebration of their loved one's life at a later occasion.

Brian Ugalde is a funeral director at Ugalde and Son in Liskeard which has its own florist

Andrew Judd from The Association of Funeral Directors said families did not always realise exactly what was included when they bought a direct cremation plan.

"The average cost of a direct cremation at the moment is Â£1,628," he said.

"So that's the cost of the person that you've lost being collected from where they died, being cremated somewhere in the country and you having the ashes delivered back to you, that's all it is."

If you were to have an attended funeral service, that can range from Â£4,200, but you have a funeral with that."

The Park has lowered the price of direct cremation

John Ware from Walter C Parsons funeral directors in Plympton suggested people talked to loved ones before buying an unattended cremation plan so their family knew what to expect.

"If they talk to their local funeral director there is probably an option that sits somewhere between a direct cremation and a completely traditional funeral which would suit the need of both parties, in terms of keeping the cost lower, but also having a service that the family can attend to reflect on that person's life."

Plymouth's The Park crematorium, which opened in 2024, has recently reduced the cost of direct cremation, citing lower prices offered by other crematoria and national companies.

Graham Smith, head of bereavement, registration and coroner services at Plymouth City Council, said national providers could use crematoria in other parts of the country meaning not everyone was cremated in their home city.

"In my view if you live in Plymouth, if you work in Plymouth, why would you use a national provider?"

The cost of traditional funeral services has increased

VAT, the cost of wood for coffins and fuel price increases have all added to the cost of traditional funeral services. But people the BBC spoke to in Plymouth had mixed views on direct cremation.

Andrew Statton said he had not previously heard of direct cremation but added: "My wife died seven years ago of cancer and we had the ashes and then had a memorial service afterwards, so it makes no difference does it?"

Ann Roberts said she would consider a direct cremation because it would save her family money and they could "decide what they want to do". She added: "If you don't talk to your family, they won't know what you want."

But Pam Brinkhurst said she preferred a more tradition funeral. "When you get cremated or buried the family are there. It's a much better send off."

Phoenix Elson agreed: "I would rather pay the extra money and for my son to actually be there to celebrate my life".

Simplicity is one of the national companies offering direct cremation and said "our nationwide network of crematoria helps us keep fees competitive".

They added: "Giving customers more choice, including the option to be cremated locally rather than having their loved one transported across the country to secure the lowest fee, is a positive development for consumers and the sector as a whole in the Plymouth area."

#219. Republicans outspent Democrats for first time in over a decade in record 2026 midterm election political ad blitz

Political advertisers spent a record $12.06 billion during the 2025-26 elections, making it the first time a midterm cycle outspent a presidential cycle, according to a report from advertising intelligence company AdImpact released Tuesday.

Total spending exceeded AdImpact's June projection by more than $460 million.

The elections also marked the first time in over a decade that Republicans have outspent Democrats on advertisements, according to AdImpact.

Despite Republicans leading spending, John Link, senior vice president of data at AdImpact, said that "Dems still led or were generally even with overall airings and impressions."

The record spending extended across the board, with every type of race reaching a new high. Senate spending hit $3.39 billion, while spending on gubernatorial elections reached $2.61 billion, spending on races in the House of Representatives grew to $2.44 billion and state legislative races totaled $602 million, all records, according to AdImpact.

The 2026 midterm cycle was largely powered by megadonors, with SpaceX and Tesla CEO Elon Musk becoming the largest contributor by funneling more than $366 million into campaigns.

The biggest donors in this year's election disproportionately favored Republicans, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records. Fourteen out of the 20 largest donor groups supported GOP candidates, according to that analysis.

Republicans spent $5.51 billion on political advertisements, compared with $5.11 billion spent by Democrats, according to AdImpact.

While Republican spending increased by 27% from 2024, spending by Democrats fell by 4%.

Fundraisers told CNBC in interviews that Democratic donors' hesitancy largely stems from memories of Kamala Harris' defeat in 2024, unease about the rise of democratic socialism and distrust in party leadership.

Though they were outspent, Democrats made sweeping gains against Republicans in races nationwide, taking control of both the House and the Senate and flipping seats in some of the most competitive places, including Texas and Kansas.

It is not the first time spending did not align with the winners. In 2024, President Donald Trump defeated former Vice President Harris despite Democrats outspending Republicans.

While party majorities in Congress have switched hands, the most popular medium for political ads has not changed.

Broadcast television represented the largest category for spending in this year's election, accounting for 49%, or $5.85 billion, of political ad spending, according to AdImpact. Broadcast's share of political ad dollars is up slightly from 48% in 2024.

Broadcast TV's strength comes despite facing pressure from falling viewership and increasing competition from streaming.

Industry experts have told CNBC that political advertisers continue to spend on broadcast because it reaches local markets and active voting populations. The Associated Press also reported that airtime was so expensive during the midterm elections that some candidates, including Republican Ken Paxton in Texas, bought ads during national sporting events, figuring that reaching enough constituents through a national broadcast might actually be less expensive than buying across the state's local television markets.

The fastest-growing categories in this year's cycle were connected TV, or internet-connected devices like Apple TV or Roku, with $2.66 billion, and digital, with $1.91 billion. Both mediums grew double digits from the 2024 presidential election. Spending on cable and radio declined 7% and 13%, respectively, from 2024.

#216. More support for households reliant on heating oil

Heating oil prices have been volatile since the conflict between the US and Iran began in March

Households using heating oil as their main energy source are being offered more financial support by North Northamptonshire Council.

Residents can apply for a Â£250 voucher in emergency situations where low oil levels would put health, safety or basic living conditions at risk.

Heating oil is not protected by the Ofgem energy price cap, which means those reliant on it feel price increases faster than those on mains gas or electricity.

The Reform UK-led council encouraged residents to see if they were eligible for support online, external.

Heating oil prices have been volatile since the conflict between the US and Iran began in March.

The government announced a Â£53m support package in response to rising costs.

Rural areas, including parts of North Northamptonshire, are among the most affected, the council said.

Greg Wilcox, its executive member for communities and rural affairs, added: "We know that times are hard for many households and we want to do all we can to support as many residents as possible."

#215. Jensen Huang's — and Nvidia's — legal exposure balloons

The still-unraveling scandal of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's extramarital affairs and the tech giant's potential role in its advanced semiconductor chips ending up in China present a legal minefield for Huang and Nvidia.

Federal and state prosecutors and Nvidia employees may target Huang and the company in court, where some shareholders have already taken action.

That's on top of a rapidly growing pile of probes by Republicans who control Congress now and Democrats who will have majorities in both the House and Senate come January.

"It's not going to get any better for Jensen Huang; it's going to get far worse," said Salvatore Graziano, a New York securities litigation attorney at Bernstein, Litowitz, Berger & Grossmann. His firm is looking at shareholder lawsuit opportunities against Nvidia and Huang.

[Huang] "faces the Department of Justice, the Securities and Exchange Commission and private litigation," Graziano said. "The criminal exposure is very serious. The SEC could bar him from being an officer. And the potential liability [from shareholder] lawsuits can be very large."

Huang's and Nvidia's legal exposure stems from three issues that led up to his shock resignation Nov. 12.

There's his alleged affair with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who worked for Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company under investigation for diverting advanced Nvidia semiconductor chips to China in violation of U.S. export rules. That, in turn, has raised questions about whether Huang and Nvidia were aware of any diversions.

There are Huang's two other alleged affairs with Nvidia employees.

And there is the sharp drop in the company's stock price in the wake of the scandal. Nvidia's share price has fallen 19% since Nov. 8, when Huang sent a company-wide apology acknowledging extramarital affairs.

Huang last week retained the law firm Paul, Weiss and its attorneys Melinda Haag and Walter Brown to represent him. Haag is a former U.S. attorney in San Francisco, and Brown is also a former federal prosecutor.

Huang's spokesman at Trident, a crisis communications firm, declined to comment when asked if Haag and Brown are representing him in any potential criminal case and other questions about his legal exposure.

Nvidia did not respond to a request for comment. Last week, Nvidia retained the law firm Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz to conduct an independent investigation into Huang allegedly being targeted by a spy, as U.S. intelligence reportedly has concluded.

The civil liability Huang and Nvidia could face is underscored by a hearing Graziano, the securities attorney, attended Tuesday in U.S. District Court in San Francisco. A judge at the hearing approved a $175 million settlement from Seagate Technology Holdings to resolve a shareholder lawsuit alleging it concealed sales of hard-disk drives to Huawei in China in violation of export bans.

The Seagate settlement came three years after the Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security — which is investigating Megaspeed's business with Nvidia — imposed a $300 million penalty on Seagate for its sales to Huawei.

Graziano said his law firm is "actively looking at securities law liability for Nvidia, Jensen Huang and other senior executives in the company given all the recent developments regarding their [potential] knowledge and facilitation of illegal chip sales to Chinese entities."

There is already a pending class-action shareholder lawsuit in San Francisco federal court against Nvidia and Huang by the Rosen Law Firm, for allegedly misleading and false statements related to Megaspeed and Alice Huang.

"Several hundreds of investors have contacted us and expressed an interest to join the case — investors of all walks of life, from mom and pops to investors with very large losses," Rosen attorney Phil Kim told CNBC in an email.

Katie Sweeten, a former federal prosecutor in the DOJ's National Security Division, said the question of "who knew what and when, and what was [Huang's] role" is potentially the biggest risk for Nvidia and him.

"Now DOJ can leverage their criminal authorities," Sweeten, a partner at Scale LLP, told CNBC's Kelly Evans on "The Exchange."

"They can bring grand jury subpoenas, search warrants for emails, bank accounts, personal devices, computers, things like that," Sweeten said.

While it is "not illegal to, you know, sleep with a spy ... it is illegal to pass certain types of information to a counterintelligence officer," she said.

Sweeten also pointed to potential export control violations, which "could be very serious."

She noted the DOJ can indict a company as well as its officials if there is evidence of criminality. Export control violations carry a maximum possible criminal sentence of 20 years in prison and "really large fines," she said.

"You can't send the company to prison, but they can face fines, civil penalties, debarment — where you can't export items down the line," she said.

One example of that came in July 2025, when the DOJ obtained a guilty plea and an agreement to pay more than $140 million from Cadence Design Systems for violating the Export Control Reform Act by unlawfully exporting semiconductor design tools to China's National University of Defense Technology, a school led by China's Central Military Commission.

Another potential legal risk for Huang comes from his planned appearances before Congress.

Huang is scheduled to give a closed-door interview Dec. 1 to three House committees. Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., wants Huang's testimony before that panel to be public, and told CNBC Tuesday he hopes to have that happen "before Christmas."

Sweeten said Huang "has to be very careful" about what he says there because of the risk of being criminally charged with lying to Congress, and of saying something "that absolutely can become part of a criminal or civil investigation."

"I expect Huang to take the Fifth on most questions" from Congress, Jonathan Kanter, a former assistant attorney general in the DOJ's Antitrust Division, told CNBC, meaning Huang will likely use his constitutional right against giving testimony that could be used against him in a criminal case.

"With a Justice Department criminal investigation into Megaspeed already underway and more investigations possible, anything he tells Congress could be used against him," Kanter said. "His lawyers are likely to advise him against taking that risk."

Ben Heineman Jr., a former senior vice president and general counsel of General Electric, and a senior fellow at Harvard University, told CNBC that Nvidia's board will be scrutinized over its actions before it pushed Huang to resign.

Heineman cited the board's apparent lack of succession planning and what looks like weak oversight of Huang, including relating to personal conduct such as the two alleged affairs with Nvidia employees and with an executive of an Nvidia customer.

"When there is a sort of reasonable suspicion, they need to investigate it, and they don't appear to have done that," Heineman said.

Nvidia and Huang could face investigation from state attorneys general, including in California, where the company is headquartered.

On Tuesday, California Attorney General Rob Bonta told Bloomberg Television that the situation at Nvidia with Huang is "something that we're interested in." Bonta's office later told CNBC, "We are monitoring the situation."

Huang's alleged affairs with Nvidia employees also open the possibility of workplace litigation.

Ari Wilkenfeld, a Washington-area employment lawyer, said a consensual relationship between a chief executive and a subordinate "is not in and of itself illegal."

"The liability never comes from the existence of the relationship alone. It comes from whether it was genuinely welcome, what job benefits flowed from it, and what it did to everyone else in the building," Wilkenfeld said. "California law recognizes that so-called sexual favoritism can create a hostile environment for an entire workforce.

"The affair is Huang's problem," Wilkenfeld said. "What, if anything, Nvidia did once it knew is the company's problem."

#212. Why are more roofs not made of solar tiles?

From installer to instructor Kate Collardson (left) has a long history in solar systems

Some people in the US "still hate the look of solar", Kate Collardson acknowledges.

She would know. Collardson handles operations for the Solar Service of Colorado, a solar maintenance and inspection company. She's also held just about every other job in the industry, from installer to instructor.

For those that hate the look of solar panels, an alternative is a solar roof, where the tiles (or shingles) house solar cells, but look much the same as regular tiles.

This is especially important for historic buildings, where heritage rules limit changes to their appearance.

For many people, the first time they'd heard of this product category was in August, when Tesla's website stopped offering solar roofs, external. This followed disappointing sales and a lawsuit over sudden price hikes, external.

Other companies have also pulled out, but Tesla's decision in particular sent shockwaves through the solar roof industry.

Some see it as a death knell for solar tiles; others, as an opportunity.

Tesla abruptly stopped marketing its solar tiles in August

For instance, the small Swiss company Freesuns is considering expanding to the US.

Deborah Learoyd, Freesuns' CEO and managing director, estimates that the company's sales have grown 20â30% since it was established in 2019.

In general, solar roof components continue to be manufactured in smaller batches. This limits the economies of scale and standardisation, though flexible manufacturing could help somewhat.

Learoyd says that while the unit costs for Freesuns' products are slowly decreasing, "a lot of the cost is actually the fact that every roof is a little bit different. And so it has to be engineered a little bit differently, installed a little bit differently".

Solar roofs typically require collaboration among roofers, solar installers and electricians. And building regulations tend to be more complex than for solar panels.

The additional time and paperwork can take a toll on a roofing business, Collardson explains. "You try to put a roofer into the equation and they're going, 'This is taking forever. I'm losing money. I could have sold 10 roofing jobs in the time that it takes me to sell this one'."

Collardson has to charge more for repair of solar shingles, compared to a standard photovoltaic (PV) setup.

She explains, "With traditional solar, we generally have electronics on the panel and a map that shows us exactly how much each panel is producing." So when something goes wrong, it's easy to spot where.

"With solar shingles, we don't really have the benefit of that mapping; there's no room underneath the shingle for the electronics." Thermal cameras can identify the trouble spots, however.

But then there's the matter of accessing them. "You can remove the one in the middle of the array, but you have to walk on the others, and you really shouldn't walk on them because you're going to create micro-fractures and that's going to cause issues down the road."

Before repair costs are factored in, a solar roof might cost twice as much as a system of solar panels mounted on a roof.

Freesuns solar tiles on the LycÃ©e Jean-Piaget in NeuchÃ¢tel, Switzerland

Solar roofs are also less efficient - one reason is that the tiles are just smaller.

Another factor is the energy lost as heat. Since the tiles sit close to the roof, there's less airflow than with standard solar panels mounted on a roof. Heat impairs solar performance.

Coloured tiles are even less efficient, comments Ben Graves, co-founder of UK solar company Sunsave. "The reason why all solar cells are black, and increasingly the whole panel is now black, is because black is the best absorbing colour."

Overall, much more space would need to be devoted to solar roof tiles to produce the same amount of energy.

Sunsave does not offer solar roofs. "Most people in the UK just don't have enough money to pay for it," Graves believes.

While appearance was a bigger factor five years ago, now "the vast majority of consumers are most interested in how much they can save".

But supporters of solar roofs argue that they should not be pitted against standard solar modules on roofs.

"Let's not compare PV roof tiles to a standard PV system, but look at PV roof tiles as generating additional electricity from our roofs instead of just having the roof sit there," urges Martin Heinrich who leads a research group at the Fraunhofer Institute for Solar Energy Systems in Germany.

A solar roof can cost twice as much as installing solar panels

While standard solar panels have gone from strength to strength, solar roofs have not advanced to the same extent.

One advancement that could especially help solar roofs is matrix shingling technology.

This is a different way of connecting solar cells. The solar cells are cut into narrow strips, layered like overlapping shingles, and connected to each other using electrically conductive adhesive, rather than metal wires.

This means that the electrical current can flow around shaded areas, unlike with standard modules. "If you get like a bird pooping on a part of the cell, the current can go around it," Learoyd explains.

In addition to increasing energy output, this improves the appearance as the silver wires aren't visible.

Another advantage, Heinrich says, is that "since we cut the cells into smaller areas, we can fill out special sizes more easily. You don't have to adhere to the regular pattern size."

Freesuns is an early adopter of matrix shingling technology in building-integrated PV. "We see it as a big opportunity for the future in terms of how we would make our tiles," Learoyd says.

What would also help the company is better software for automated design. This would speed up the design process, since each roofing job is unique.

Learoyd believes this would lower costs and give "the whole construction industry much more confidence in using the product".

Ten years after her own solar roof was installed, and despite the sector's setbacks, Learoyd remains optimistic.

She believes that for people with the means, it will eventually become standard to install a new roof with some sort of embedded solar. "It's just a no-brainer."

The critical tech staying safe by going underground

#209. How long will humans be needed for translation?

Google's translation service handles one trillion words per month

"Il pleut des cordes," say the French when it's raining hard.

"It's raining ropes," is the direct translation. It's the kind of idiom which can trip up an AI translator.

"You can imagine the dilemma this throws AI into," says FranÃ§ois Yvon, a research director specialised in automatic translation with the French National Centre for Scientific Research CNRS.

He says a well-trained system will know that ropes are not literally falling from the sky and use the English phrase "it's raining cats and dogs".

"But is it going to find the equivalent when it's translating into Indonesian?" asks Yvon.

A comment on the weather is pretty low-risk, but more and more translation is being done by machines, and on topics with higher stakes than the weather.

In April, Google said people are now translating, external one trillion words a month with Google Translate.

That doesn't count the others: DeepL, Microsoft, Claude, ChatGPT and Gemini, not to mention made-to-measure corporate translation systems.

Direct sales of machine translation, external (i.e. outside general AI subscriptions) were worth an estimated $1.4bn (Â£1bn) last year.

AI translation is the "right call" in many cases says Jonathan Zimmermann

"For plenty of use cases, raw AI translation is now the right call: multiplying your costs by 100 to catch the last few percent of errors isn't rational if you're translating internal documents or product listings," says Jonathan Zimmermann, founder of OKA (Open Knowledge Association), a Swiss non-profit that translates Wikipedia articles into other languages.

"For an encyclopedia it's different. Wikipedia runs on trust. A wrong fact can sit there for years and get cited by journalists and AI systems," he says.

With more than 10,000 published pieces, mostly into Spanish, Portuguese and English, OKA is, Zimmerman says, the single largest contributor of translated articles to the collaborative online encyclopedia.

Which is something it absolutely could not have become without AI.

"Translating 10,000 articles the traditional way would cost millions. In our process, the AI produces the draft, then a human translator checks every sentence," he explains.

But that human input is required less and less.

"The machine output is now good enough that skipping the human entirely has become a serious option, and a real temptation across the industry. Two or three years ago, with tools like DeepL, our translators still rewrote a good chunk of every draft.

"Today the prose often doesn't need fixing at all. So, the question is no longer whether AI translation is good, it clearly is, but whether it's good enough to remove the human," says Zimmermann.

Ariella BaltiÃ© is a translator and legal expert specialised in translating court documents from English into French.

She was contacted recently by someone who'd already translated his documents with AI, asking her to check them.

"There were two major errors right at the beginning," she said.

The AI had translated "Le demandeur" (the plaintiff) as "the requester". "Totally awkward for legal papers," says BaltiÃ©.

When the AI saw "Madame X, Ã©pouse Y" (Ms X, wife of Y'), it came up with "Ms X marries Y".

In other words, it mistook a noun for a verb, completely losing the meaning of the phrase.

The consequences of mistranslation in the legal sphere can be serious. In the US, for example, errors in machine-translated asylum applications have led to delays in the release of detainees.

Ariella BaltiÃ© specialises in legal translation work

Accuracy is a matter of life and death in the medical field.

"The units for dosages and blood tests often include Greek letters," says Carol Bereuter, who is a specialist in medical translations.

She came across one automatically-translated text that had translated the greek letter Î¼ as milli (one thousandth) instead of micro (one millionth).

"That's a serious error. If you give someone a milligram when it's meant to be a microgram, you are giving them a thousand times the dose," points out Bereuter.

She also makes the point that, as AI translators have got better, catching errors has got more difficult.

"Whereas in the past, with things like Google Translate, the sentence would jump out at you because there'd be a grammatical error or it would sound clunky, today it sounds perfectly legitimate. It sounds fluid. It sounds natural. So the errors are a lot harder to spot."

Carol Bereuter is a specialist in medical translations

"If it's something that will bear your signature, there's a risk for your reputation. And if decisions are going to be taken on the basis of a translation concerning a medical procedure, a contract you might sign or a major purchase you might make, the risk is even greater," warns FranÃ§ois Yvon.

Even sentences that seem quite clear to a human can trip up an AI service, he says.

"When you have words with different meanings, it's usually very clear for a human which one applies because they understand the context, but when a machine translator comes across the sentence 'translate into Polish', for example, it is quite capable of deciding it must be all about shoe cream, says Yvon.

Context is often crucial. If an AI translator hasn't grasped that we're talking about a football match it is liable to mis-translate "taking a corner".

Last year OKA ran a study, funded by Wikimedia. It studied 119 articles, 10 language pairs and more than 1,000 hours of translator work.

"27% of the AI-generated text still got corrected before publication. Three quarters of those corrections were errors the AI introduced, and 5 to 6% of errors changed the meaning of the original text," says Zimmerman.

Eradicating those errors is likely to be challenging.

"As things stand, I think the fundamental way these systems function makes it quite hard to imagine how they could become 100% reliable," says FranÃ§ois Yvon.

This is because probability is a crucial part of the way AI translates each word rather than another and what is probably correct is not the same as what is correct. Especially in the realm of something as subtle and surprising as language.

He thinks a new generation of tech will be an improvement.

"We will see translation systems that are increasingly capable of evaluating what they do and correcting themselves using different AI tools simultaneously: one to do the translation, a second to criticize it and a third to produce a new version, for example."

But perhaps the big moment will be when AI can show doubt.

"The real breakthrough won't come until these systems are not merely capable of translating but also of telling us when they're not sure," Yvon says.

#206. Netflix, Amazon, Fox to bid for 2030 men's World Cup rights; Disney out

Netflix, Amazon, YouTube and Fox are all expected to submit offers to win the U.S. media rights for the 2030 men's World Cup before today's deadline, according to people familiar with the matter — but Disney will not be putting in a bid.

Fox and NBCU's Telemundo are the incumbent rights holders. FIFA would prefer to sell both the English and Spanish rights together, to one buyer, for the 2030 World Cup, to be held in Spain, Portugal and Morocco. Media companies also have the right to bid on the 2034 World Cup, which will be held in Saudi Arabia.

CNBC previously reported that media executives are planning on the winning bid coming in around $1.5 billion — or maybe even higher — although that amount could vary somewhat depending on whether both 2030 and 2034 are acquired.

FIFA is open to the idea of a potential partnership bid in which rights are split between a traditional broadcaster and a streamer, to maximize reach. Still, unlike in other countries where there are government mandates on airing games for free, FIFA will not mandate that a subscription streamer sublicense games. That decision will be up to the winning bidder.

FIFA is satisfied that enough people in the U.S. already have a Netflix or Amazon Prime Video subscription that reach won't be an issue, according to people familiar with the matter. The 2027 Women's World Cup will be on Netflix in the U.S., and Netflix plans to air all of the games without a partner.

Disney/ESPN will not be bidding, according to people familiar with the matter. ESPN Chairman Jimmy Pitaro said he was interested in World Cup rights earlier this year at CNBC Sport x Boardroom's Game Plan Summit.

In recent weeks, ESPN ran the numbers and couldn't get to $1.5 billion or higher while also having to save money for NHL, MLB and European soccer — English Premier League and Champions League — all of which will likely renew their media rights deals in 2027, said the people, who asked not to be named because the discussions were private. ESPN looked into entering a bid with a partner but ultimately decided not to bid, one of the people said.

NBCUniversal still hasn't made a decision on bidding, potentially putting Telemundo in jeopardy of losing the games for 2030, according to a person familiar with the matter. NBCUniversal has already spent billions on NBA and also needs to save money for both MLB and EPL potentially as well as the NFL, which will likely be a 2028 negotiation.

Skydance and Apple will not bid, according to people familiar with the matter.

A FIFA spokesperson declined to comment, citing confidentiality of the tender process.

FIFA executives plan to travel to the U.S. next week to meet personally with bidders to assess their bids and potentially get final offers. FIFA plans to choose a winner before the end of the year.

#204. As Christmas rush looms, how do China-made toys find their way to the US?

China is the world's largest toymaker, producing the majority of toys on sale in the US

On the underside of most children's toys on sale in the US you'll find the same three words â "Made in China".

Nearly eight in 10 toys (78.3%) imported into the country in 2024 came from China, according to US government figures. , external

Last year, toys imported from China were worth $9.8bn (Â£7.4bn), trade figures show. , external

With Christmas on the horizon, the toy industry in the US and elsewhere is about to have its busiest time of the year by far.

As retailers prepare their stocks for the festive rush, be they online giants or "mom-and-pop" physical stores, the BBC World Service's Follow the Money series traces how a toy gets from a factory in China to the US. And where the money goes.

Guangdong province, which neighbours Hong Kong in southern China, is the heart of the country's toy manufacturing industry.

In the city of Dongguan workers on the floor of the Mihox factory pop plastic toys out of steel moulds, and pile them into crates ready for painting.

The company says it employs more than 800 people and makes around 55 million toys a year for the American market, which represents half of its business.

Follow the money: The journey of a $30 toy from China to America

Every toy starts with a design from a customer, such as an American toy company. The factory then makes a prototype, and builds moulds from which plastic parts and other components are formed.

Going from the first idea to finished toy takes one to two years, says Melanie Wang from the Mihox sales team. Much of that is the customer going back to the drawing board. "They modified the design again, again, and again to improve it."

Once the design is signed off, things move more quickly. A mould takes about a month to make, and the production run lasts about three months.

The Mihox factory in Dongguan employs more than 800 people

So, how much of the US retail price does the factory get? "About 20%," says Wang.

On a $30 toy, that's $6, which has to cover the price of the plastic and other materials, production costs including wages, and what profit the factory makes.

Chinese factories typically make a profit of between 3% and 10%, according to Jay Foreman, chief executive of US toy firm Basic Fun. So for a $30 toy that's between 60 and 18 cents of the $6 the factory charges to make it.

Samson Ko is president of the Toys Manufacturers' Association of Hong Kong. Many of his members moved their factories across the border into mainland China in the 1980s, attracted by cheaper land and labour.

He says the way toys are made has barely changed since then. "Automation now takes place mostly in the moulding and the packaging process. But for soft toys, or even some of the electronics nowadays, it's still very labour intensive."

And Chinese labour isn't cheap anymore, he adds, after recent rises.

Melanie Wang explains that Mihox gets 20% of a toy's retail price

Average pay in Vietnam is about half of that in China, said one report, external at the start of this year.

Yet fewer than 10% of Ko's members have moved any production outside China, and in those cases mostly only because customers asked them to do so.

The Chinese factories remain busy, he says, because a single toy can undergo more than one manufacturing process, from moulding the parts, to adding electronics, sewing materials, and final assembly. This often requires it travelling to different factories.

Ko adds that in Guangdong these separate facilities are located close to each other, and used to "working in harmony". He says this makes the whole manufacturing process run more smoothly than in other countries.

From the factory, the toy goes by truck to a port, such as Shenzhen, the biggest in Guangdong.

Ivy Tse is co-founder of FreightAmigo, a Hong Kong logistics company that runs an online platform for both Hong Kong and mainland China exporters. These businesses can book a cargo container, sea passage to an overseas destination, arrange insurance cover, and secure finance.

Tse says it takes about 15 days for a cargo ship to get from Shenzhen to the US west coast, and 25 to 30 days to the east coast. This year's Christmas toys left China in August and September.

Freight, from factory to the US, usually adds about 10% to the manufacturing cost of a toy, adds Tse. So if we stick to a toy that retails for $30 in the US, and therefore costs $6 to make in China, the cost of getting it to the states is 60 cents.

Once the toy reaches the US there is currently a tariff on Chinese toys that has to be paid to the US Customs and Border Protection (CBP).

Following talks between President Trump and Chinese leader Xi Jinping last month, the two governments have recommended toys for lower tariffs. But no new rate or start date has been set, and the US must first hold a public consultation. For now the basic rate is 12.5%, and some toys pay more.

This is charged on the manufacturing price of the toy, not on how much it retails for. So for a $30 toy, that basic rate is 12.5% of $6, which is 75 cents.

This tariff is paid for by the American company that has imported the toys. One such firm is Learning Resources, a family business based near Chicago that sells educational toys. Half of these it imports from China.

"We pay 100% of the tariffs, let there be no doubt about that," says the company's chief executive Rick Woldenberg.

Jay Foreman says that most of the money spent on a toy in the US doesn't leave the country

The cost of making the $30 toy, getting it to the US, and paying the import tariff, comes to $7.35. So that leaves $22.65, or 75.5% of the total.

That money never leaves the US, explains Foreman from Basic Fun. His firm, which is based in Florida, makes Tonka trucks and Care Bears cuddly toys.

"It's kept by my company, the [US] transportation company, the retailer," he says.

If we allow $2.65 for the US delivery firm, the toy company and the retailer will typically share the remaining revenues 50-50. So that would give them $10 each.

While profit margins are commercially sensitive and therefore not generally revealed, industry studies show that the toy firm and retailer would usually make a profit of up to 25% or $2.50.

Getting a toy onto the shelves in the US requires both a lot of work and a long journey.
