#176. Robot vessels take on the dangerous work of mine hunting

This South Korean vessel is among the ships damaged in the Strait of Hormuz

Traversing the Strait of Hormuz remains one of the most dangerous trips a vessel can take.

Despite assistance from the US Navy, ships are still being attacked.

"To transit the Strait of Hormuz you need agreement between the captain and the crew to take the risk. You need war risk insurance. And the captain has to make the final decision," says Steve Cameron, a former merchant navy officer and maritime consultant.

Missiles and drones are a threat from the air, but under the surface mines may also be lurking.

"If you're a captain, you'd like to see evidence mines aren't present," Cameron adds.

Modern mines rest on the seabed and detonate when they detect an acoustic or magnetic signal, triggering a pressure wave that will break the keel of a passing vessel.

Others can rise through shallow waters or float, or be powered towards a target.

The technology itself is not difficult or costly, but the mere threat can stop a tanker fleet from sailing.

Ian McFarlane of French defence group Thales is a naval architect who knows mine warfare well. "Mines are a very cheap way of waging a naval war, as the Royal Navy is acutely aware," he says.

Finding sea mines has traditionally been the job of specialist mine-hunting vessels, with crews of around 80 sailors.

To keep humans out of harm's way, an increasing amount of the work is done by systems that are autonomous and monitored from the shore.

They might only need 20 personnel, working in shifts on land. With the recruitment and retention challenges facing Nato navies these highly-portable robot systems are very attractive.

Such tech has been under development for 20 years.

During the 2003 Iraq war the US Navy poked around the seabed looking for mines with a torpedo-shaped robot called the Remus 100.

Pinging around with sonar these little machines operated down to around 100ft depth and spared divers a difficult and dangerous task.

Iran's military published this picture of a US submersible captured in the Gulf

The latest conflict in the Gulf is testing much more capable systems.

The US military is sparing with details about their mine-hunting craft, but we have seen glimpses of them.

Iranian forces hauled an uncrewed US autonomous sub, external out of the waters of the Strait of Hormuz in early September.

The Pentagon claimed this robot, an Extra Large Uncrewed Underwater Vehicle (XLUUV) from US autonomous defence tech venture Anduril, was a malfunctioning older device with no classified equipment on-board.

Whatever its mission, no sailors were imperilled. That, says William Lupton, a naval analyst with Shephard Group, "was a drop in the ocean compared to having prisoners taken", and part of the point of uncrewed vessels.

What if an uncrewed vessel is captured? Nato navies are reluctant to go into details, but computer drives can be wiped or destroyed remotely by various means. This may have taken place inside the humming circuit boards of the XLUUV seized by Iran.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle is packed with sensors

Such autonomous tech is being embraced by forces around the world.

Cooperation between Thales, the Royal Navy, and France's Marine Nationale has produced a robot motor launch.

The Thales Uncrewed Surface Vehicle (USV) is packed with sensors programmed to detect mines via sonar that was developed for the oil and gas industries.

It tows a 5m long rectangular box, similar to a large torpedo, that takes a hi-resolution look at the seabed and creates a 3D picture refined by AI. The latest mines are designed to blend into their surroundings and the software spots anything that looks like a mine.

Autonomous systems are cheaper than conventional minehunters, plus they can be deployed, along with containerised control centres, by air.

Steve Prest, associate fellow at the RUSI defence think tank, introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy and was its first fleet robotics officer, a title he acquired in 2014.

"There is a bunch of ways to look for a mine using robot boats or robot subs, and while you can't prove the negative, that no mines are there, you can reduce the risk."

He says US robot minehunters can either blow up a mine or just mark the spot as somewhere to avoid.

"We'll see more use of autonomy but also the missions and capability will expand. And AI will be analysing data from those vessels on the spot as opposed to data being analysed when a drone returns."

The Royal Navy recently announced its concept of a hybrid navy - mixing drones with conventional warships. But Prest thinks for this vision to succeed the fleet must operate as one distributed force, sharing data in what he terms a cognitive architecture.

The robot only makes a real difference as part of this scheme and it's a mistake to focus on individual uncrewed assets. "Uncrewed or autonomous vessels are not the operating system. They are applications running on it."

Steve Prest introduced uncrewed mine hunting into the Royal Navy

While robots are on the rise, people are what really matter in the Strait.

Neil Roberts speaks for insurers at Lloyd's of London on marine matters and says the psychological impact of the mine threat is inescapable, as is the uncertainty around just how many mines, if any, are out there.

"The US says it has cleared the Omani route and believes the mine threat is being managed, with shipments running at about 75% of pre-conflict levels."

Fear of the unknown is the most potent aspect of the sea mine threat and robots are now the weapon of choice to confront that fear.

#174. The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

BBC's Samira Hussain reports from Wall Street on what the fall of the tech superstar means.

Jensen Huang, known for sporting a black leather jacket to shareholder events, appeared to have it all. He was courted by leaders in the US and China alike, worshipped by investors and seen as a loyal family man. But a rockstar approach to his personal life has ultimately led to his resignation.

The 63-year-old has been married since 1985, long before the foundation of Nvidia, the company that propelled him to tech stardom.

He is known as a workaholic and a demanding manager, with high standards for those who worked for him, which included his own children, promoted within the company. But he has seen his personal reputation go into freefall this week, undone by romantic entanglements that throw doubt on his focus and his judgement.

On Thursday, he resigned following back-to-back revelations of two affairs. News of the first - a relationship with a businesswoman alleged to be a Chinese spy - ricocheted around the world, raising eyebrows among investors. It was followed on Wednesday by reports that the tech boss had also had an affair with an Nvidia colleague.

His departure creates uncertainty in the global stock market, which was already knocked-off balance in recent weeks. Nvidia is a linchpin in the US's race for technology leadership with China.

Huang may not be a household name on the same level as Elon Musk or Mark Zuckerberg.

But his role in the tech industry, especially in relation to the explosion of artificial intelligence in the last three years, may prove more consequential. Nvidia dominates the market for the microchips that are used to power the new technology.

The company, which is still valued at nearly $4n (Â£3.02tn) despite recent events, is at the centre of the AI race between the US and China, the world's tech and economic powerhouses.

Huang enjoyed access to the seats of political power on an unrivalled scale, being courted by both US President Donald Trump and China's President Xi Jinping.

His remarkable rise to the top of his profession has led to him being dubbed the "Taylor Swift of tech" and seen him amass an estimated fortune of $145bn, according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

Despite always donning his trademark all-black outfits and taking a playful approach to investor presentations, Huang has worked for years to present himself as a family man, someone who exemplified the American Dream.

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang and his family moved to the west coast of the US when he was nine years old. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a Master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

It was at college where he met his wife, Lori Mills. The pair were lab partners and married in 1985, eight years before Huang founded Nvidia.

They have two children, Madison and Spencer, who now both work in senior roles at Nvidia.

The company became a key provider of the computer chips and circuits used for gaming before it expanded in a new direction. It now creates the key processing chips used to power the growing AI technology.

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang resigns after tech giant rocked by second affair allegation

Who could replace Jensen Huang as Nvidia's chief executive?

Huang has hailed AI as a "new industrial revolution" that is on par with the advent of electricity. Nvidia is the world's second most valuable company and its customers include the biggest firms on the planet, Google, Meta, Amazon, Microsoft, SpaceX, Tesla, OpenAI and Anthropic, all spending big on its products.

So it is no surprise that at a recent AI summit at the White House, Huang was picked out from a group of 42 to flank the president, alongside Elon Musk, both men with whom Trump has famously close relationships.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Jensen Huang, told the BBC, referring to the late boss of Apple. He added that Huang was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

He described him as a "demanding" boss, who "really pushes his people to the limit".

But while the former Nvidia CEO is "very brilliant" and a "world-class computer scientist", there's also "a level of arrogance" and "a level of self-deception as well", he said.

Huang had gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, he added, something that was "very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," Witt added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

First reports emerged on 29 October in the New York Times that Huang had conducted a relationship with Alice Huang, no relation. US officials allege the businesswoman associated with Singapore-based firm Megaspeed is a Chinese intelligence asset.

After the revelations of the first affair, Huang admitted to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting their family.

Trump, as well as other leaders in the AI tech industry, initially backed Huang to continue in his role at Nvidia. The president described him as "honourable".

But the reporting of a second affair, with Kimberly Powell, vice president of healthcare who has worked at Nvidia for 18 years, prompted a board meeting, followed by the announcement Huang was stepping down.

In the aftermath of that news Trump said Huang had done "a fantastic job" and proposed Tim Cook of Apple as a suitable replacement at the head of Nvidia.

#171. How a sex scandal at Nvidia is putting pressure on the White House

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

How close is Donald Trump to Jensen Huang?

The Nvidia boss was one of the few business leaders with direct access to the US president. He had met Trump repeatedly, travelled with him to Britain and, in May, joined him on Air Force One for his trip to China.

Jensen Huang wasn't just another tech billionaire seeking the president's ear. He had become an important voice in one of the biggest arguments inside the administration: how much of America's most advanced technology should be allowed to reach China.

Nvidia makes the chips that power the AI revolution. Washington has restricted exports of its most advanced processors to China since 2022, believing they could help Beijing develop its military and technological capabilities.

But enforcing those restrictions is difficult. Chips can be bought legally by customers in countries such as Singapore and Malaysia and then diverted to China. Chinese companies can also rent access to computing power through data centres outside the country.

Jensen Huang has long argued that the restrictions are counterproductive. They shut Nvidia out of a huge market and encourage Chinese companies to develop their own alternatives. Trump appeared sympathetic to this view, allowing Nvidia to resume sales of certain chips to China last year in return for a share of the revenue, although its most advanced Blackwell chips remain off limits.

But that relationship now looks rather more complicated.

The allegations surrounding Jensen Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, of no relation but a former executive at Singapore-based company Megaspeed, have raised the possibility that a foreign intelligence operation was targeting not just a powerful businessman, but someone with unusually close access to the American president.

US intelligence reportedly suspects Alice Huang of having links to Chinese intelligence. Beijing has rejected the allegations. Nvidia's board says it has no reason to believe company secrets or national security were compromised. But the affair has opened up a much bigger question: what did the administration in Washington know, and what did it do about it?

That question leads directly to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick.

His department was responsible for controlling exports of sensitive American technology. It was also being pressed to act on concerns about Megaspeed, an Nvidia customer suspected of helping divert advanced chips to China.

On Thursday, Megaspeed chief executive, Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan, said the company "takes its compliance obligations under US export control laws extremely seriously" and is "committed to working constructively and transparently with the US government". He said the company had co-operated with US Commerce Department inspections.

A highly classified memo was shared with Lutnick about the concerns surrounding Alice Huang and Megaspeed. He resisted taking action against the company, a decision that is now under scrutiny.

Other senior officials wanted a tougher response.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent was among those who reportedly questioned why Megaspeed had not been placed on the Commerce Department's blacklist.

On Friday, Bessent pointed the finger at Commerce for not blacklisting the company, telling CNBC that several government agencies "were in favour of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months".

"But Commerce has the pen... and you will have to ask them about their internal process," he added.

There have also been questions about why an investigation into the Singapore-based company was stopped after Nvidia intervened. The New York Times reported that Nvidia's general counsel contacted Commerce Department investigators probing suspected chip smuggling by Megaspeed invoking a senior Trump appointee. The department allegedly then froze its investigation and handed the case to the Justice Department.

This apparently highlighted a significant disagreement about the direction of Trump's China policy. Huang wanted access to the Chinese market. Other officials were supposedly worried about the national security consequences. Lutnick was caught in the middle.

Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

Bessent blames Lutnick's Commerce Department for failing to block Megaspeed

Members of Congress from both parties want answers. Democrats have launched an investigation into how the Commerce Department vetted Chinese companies approved to buy AI chips. Republicans are also questioning the administration's approach.

And Jensen Huang's departure has not eased the pressure.

Republican representative John Moolenaar, who chairs the House Select Committee on China, said it was in the national interest for Nvidia and other American AI firms to succeed, but that companies "should never jeopardise our nation's security" by giving Chinese firms access to their most advanced chips.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, said Huang's resignation did not absolve him of responsibility, and urged Lutnick to explain why export control loopholes remain open.

Meanwhile Jensen Huang's team told Republican lawmakers on Wednesday that he plans to cooperate with their request to testify behind closed doors.

The White House has not responded to the BBC's request for comment.

It has, however, told the New York Post that Trump has complete confidence in Lutnick, describing him as "the most transformative commerce secretary in modern history".

That is a fairly emphatic show of support, particularly amid reports that officials are weighing whether Lutnick should be forced out.

There are some obvious questions. If Lutnick had indeed been warned about the risks, why did his department fail to act? And if other senior officials were indeed pressing for action, why did it take so long?

The BBC has contacted the Commerce Department for comment. But there is an even bigger question for Trump.

The president cultivated a close relationship with Huang. He was directly involved in decisions about chip sales to China. And, according to reporting in the New York Times, he was briefed on the intelligence concerns.

This is becoming difficult to dismiss as the misjudgement of one businessman or one government department.

Jensen Huang's personal life may have triggered the crisis, but the questions now reach into the White House itself. How much influence did he have? What did the president know? And who was ultimately responsible for protecting America's most sensitive technology?

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

#168. Anthropic IPO: Investors who flip the stock may face brokerage penalty

Retail investors with access to Anthropic's initial public offering who sell the stock quickly after buying could get penalized by their brokerage firm.

Anthropic said in an IPO prospectus that it expected to make shares available to retail investors through various brokerages, including Charles Schwab, Fidelity, Robinhood, SoFi, E*Trade and some of their respective affiliates.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of Anthropic's IPO and Nasdaq debut

These broker-dealers have different "anti-flipping" policies meant to discourage investors who purchase IPO shares — including those of Anthropic — from dumping them quickly. Charles Schwab is the only one that doesn't have such a policy in place.

"One purpose is to discourage 'flippers' who are just buying with the hope of making a quick profit," Jay Ritter, director of The IPO Initiative at the University of Florida, said in an email. "Their selling could put downward pressure on the price once trading starts."

Investors who run afoul of anti-flipping rules may be prohibited from accessing future IPOs, perhaps for several months or even more than a year for repeat offenders. In the worst case, the brokerage may bar them from all future IPOs.

These policies apply only to retail investors who place an order with their brokerage firm to buy the Anthropic IPO at its offering price — something many investors don't have access to.

For example, Fidelity customers are eligible to participate in the Anthropic IPO if they have at least $25,000 in certain assets, the firm said. Even then, customers are "evaluated and ranked" based on factors like account assets to determine who can access limited shares that may be available, it said.

Importantly, anti-flipping policies don't apply to retail investors who access shares later, once Anthropic stock starts trading on a public exchange, Ritter said.

Of the retail brokerage firms listed in Anthropic's prospectus, Charles Schwab appears to be the only one without an explicit anti-flipping policy.

"At this time, Schwab does not apply a flipping restriction to any IPOs unless required by an issuer," Margaret Farrell, a company spokesperson, wrote in an email. "This is subject to change at Schwab's discretion."

She confirmed that an anti-flipping policy doesn't apply to the Anthropic IPO.

Here's how they could penalize IPO flippers.

E*TRADE said it "prefers investors hold shares for at least 30 calendar days."

However, its policy doesn't lay out an explicit penalty for failing to do so.

Selling or transferring out of an IPO within 30 days "may affect eligibility to participate in other new issues in the future," according to its policy. "E*TRADE reserves the right to exclude customers from future participation," it added.

Investors who are allocated Anthropic shares and sell within the first 15 calendar days from the start of trading in the secondary market won't be able to participate in future stock IPOs through Fidelity for a defined period of time, it said:

"The first day clients can sell without being labeled a 'flipper' is the 16th calendar day after the IPO trades," Fidelity said.

Robinhood's policy stipulates that selling IPO shares within 30 days of the IPO is considered flipping.

Such investors "may be prevented from participating in IPO access for 60 days," it said.

SoFi said that selling an IPO position within the first 30 calendar days after trading starts "may affect your ability to participate in future IPOs on SoFi."

#166. Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

If you're one of the nearly one million people aged 16-24 not in education, employment or training - otherwise known as "Neet" - then you might be wondering where to start.

Under-25s should be guaranteed job placements or training to help them find permanent work, a major review aimed at cutting the number of Neets will say.

But how many jobs are there where you live? Use our look-up tool to find out.

Burnham wants Neets report to be watershed moment, No 10 says, as Tories criticise welfare and tax proposals

Where you live will often have a big impact on your ability to find a job.

The Institute for Public Policy Research (IPPR) think tank used online job adverts to help identify the number of vacancies suitable for young people in the area they live, relative to the size of the local population.

It used AI to help identify the jobs, which were broadly defined as roles requiring relatively low experience or education.

According to its analysis, South Oxfordshire had 387 suitable vacancies per 1,000 18 to 24-year-olds in the area - the most of any local authority. Rotherham had the least, at 53 per 1,000.

Use this tool to check out the situation in your area. The data for London is only available for the region as a whole and is not broken down by individual councils.

Some people live in one area and work in another, but the analysis did not account for this.

Even if there are jobs available in your area, you might not find one in the sector you want to work in.

The sector with the highest average number of entry-level vacancies in the year to October was teaching, with most of those jobs being for teaching assistants, learning support and cover supervisor roles, according to jobs website Adzuna.

The next largest was hospitality and catering, traditionally an area where younger people get their first experience of work.

Adzuna, a major online jobs website, includes adverts for full-time roles marked as entry-level, those asking for up to two years' experience, graduate roles, internships and apprenticeships.

Accounting and finance had the third-highest number of job openings.

Figures on salaries for vacancies can be patchy because many employers do not include them in job adverts.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham recently urged businesses to start publishing salaries ahead of plans to make pay details compulsory in adverts.

Only about 40% of Adzuna's jobs data for the year to October stated a salary, although that figure varies widely by sector.

Here are the average salaries for jobs in the sectors with the largest number of entry-level jobs, according to Adzuna. The highest average salary is Â£30,530 in engineering, while social care has the lowest at Â£25,880.

While it can be hard for anyone looking for their first job, the situation is also tough for older jobseekers.

Across the whole economy there were 690,000 jobs available in the August to October period this year, which is the lowest number since early 2021.

The Covid pandemic led to huge swings in the number of job vacancies available as companies reacted to shutdowns and then reopened as restrictions were lifted.

Stripping out the fluctuations seen during the pandemic, the number of vacancies is now at its lowest level for 12 years.

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

Apprenticeships have often been a way for people to get their first taste of employment.

In England the number of people starting apprenticeships has risen in recent years, although this has been driven by an increase in those aged 25 and over after schemes for older workers were fully rolled out in 2008.

The number of people aged 24 and under starting apprenticeships in England in 2025 was 172,230, down from a peak of 291,300 in 2012.

#163. As Trump and Xi meet, China's AI dreams face an unexpected hurdle: the Nvidia spy scandal

Jensen Huang is out but his shadow looms over the upcoming meeting between US President Donald Trump and his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping this week.

For Beijing, the former Nvidia CEO's dramatic fall is a major challenge - and not just because of claims that a Chinese spy may have honey-trapped Huang, one of the artificial intelligence industry's most influential figures.

Rather, the allegations surrounding Huang and his affair with Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang are turning into a new flashpoint in an already fragile US-China relationship.

US intelligence reports claim she had ties to Beijing's intelligence apparatus - in particular the Ministry of State Security, China's main civilian spy agency.

The claim remains unproven, but it has already reignited debate in Washington over exports of advanced chips to China and whether the Trump administration has been too willing to ease restrictions.

For years now, advanced computer chips have been a key battleground in the US-China rivalry because they are essential for AI, supercomputing and military technology.

Chinese officials are worried this scandal could strengthen voices in Washington who argue in favour of further limiting Chinese access to cutting-edge American tech.

Just this week, the Trump administration introduced new export restrictions on Chinese firms that access advanced US chips in third countries such as Malaysia, Thailand or Brazil. They typically do this by renting computing power in data centres abroad to train their AI models. But some of the firms, like the one Alice Huang worked for, are also accused of smuggling chips to China, although they have denied this.

Until now, this controversy appeared to be a sideshow in US-China relations. But with Beijing calling the new rules "economic coercion" on the eve of Trump arriving in Shenzhen, there is a growing risk it could become something much bigger.

Watch: Jensen Huang speaks to the BBC's Faisal Islam in 2025

Democrats, who will control Congress come January, have made their position clear. Last Wednesday top Democrat Ted Lieu told Politico the Trump administration had been "1,000%" too deferential to Nvidia, putting national security at risk, while fellow Democrat Zoe Lofgren suggested Congress would now be more likely to restrict sales of AI chips to China.

The BBC has approached the White House for comment.

Beijing's foreign ministry had described the spy claims as a "smear against China".

"The US should stop fabricating so-called espionage narratives to find pretexts for imposing technological blockades," spokesperson Mao Ning said last week.

The ministry also seemed keen to keep the meeting on track, saying the two countries had kept "close communication regarding interactions between the two heads of state".

For the last year, Trump and Xi have been seeking to stabilise relations after a damaging tariff war that almost brought trade between the two countries to a standstill.

The Trump administration's decision to permit sales of advanced Nvidia H200 chips to China in late 2025 was seen as evidence that both sides were searching for areas of co-operation, even while competition intensified.

It was a rare move given Washington had spent years trying to limit China's access to the most advanced chips.

Many in the US have long viewed these chips as a national security issue, arguing they could strengthen China's military and surveillance capabilities. Jensen Huang, who rejects that claim, advocated lifting bans on the export of high-performing chips to China. Beijing argues the restrictions are an attempt to contain its rise.

History has taught Beijing and Washington that spy scandals rarely remain confined to the intelligence world.

Analysts in China already see echoes of an incident in 2023 when a suspected surveillance balloon drifted through the skies over the US before an American fighter jet shot it down. Beijing insisted it was a civilian meteorological airship that had strayed off course.

The suspected Chinese spy balloon was shot down days after it was spotted over the US

But a planned trip by then US Secretary of State Antony Blinken was cancelled and communication between the two governments deteriorated sharply.

The incident showed how quickly a security dispute could overwhelm diplomatic efforts and push relations into a deep freeze.

"The mere passage of a balloon sent American public opinion and politicians into a state of high alert, as if a "Chinese threat" were looming directly overhead," said the state media outlet, the Global Times this week as it made a direct comparison with the Nvidia scandal.

"No-one on the US side has ever apologised or taken responsibility for the genuine damage this caused to US-China relations," it added.

The state-run outlet also questioned why the Nvidia story had emerged so soon after "the hard-won detente" between Beijing and Washington, following a meeting between the two countries' leaders in September.

"Is someone behind the scenes pushing an agenda and stirring up confrontation?" it asked.

Both Trump and Xi have significant incentives to keep talking.

China's economy remains under pressure and is increasingly reliant on exports. Xi needs trade to keep flowing and this meeting is a key stepping stone to replacing the current trade truce that is due to expire in January.

Meanwhile Trump has repeatedly touted his close relationship with Xi and argued that maintaining dialogue is essential to managing competition between the two powers.

The US president is in his last two years and faces an uphill struggle to pass domestic legislation after the midterms. To counter this, he could be looking for some foreign policy wins.

He may also hope for Xi's help to end the war in the Middle East.

Neither side will want to be discussing intelligence concerns and technology controls instead of trade, investment and economic co-operation.

The summit is also being carefully choreographed by Beijing to show off its technological prowess in Shenzhen - a deeply symbolic choice of city.

It's home to some of the country's biggest tech companies and has become a showcase for Beijing's ambitions in AI and advanced manufacturing.

Also, given Washington's tighter restrictions on advanced chips, Beijing has options for retaliation.

One of its most powerful tools is its dominance of key rare earth minerals. China processes much of the world's supply for materials that are used in everything from electric vehicles and smartphones to military equipment.

Analysts have long warned that technological restrictions can trigger a cycle of retaliation in which each side reaches for its strongest economic leverage.

That would shift the current trend of US-China relations from engagement back towards mutual suspicion.

#159. Waymo pulls driverless taxis from all US freeways

Waymo has temporarily suspended its driverless vehicles from operating on all US freeways, the company has said.

The decision comes after California banned Waymo's driverless taxis from state freeways following a series of recent traffic incidents.

California's Department of Motor Vehicles (DMV) cited an "imminent hazard to public safety" for the ban, noting an incident this month in which several driverless Waymo vehicles entered an active construction zone on US Route 101.

Waymo said it was working with state officials "diligently and responsibly to resume freeway operations", adding that no injuries or collisions were reported following the incident on US Route 101 south of San Francisco.

California's DMV said Waymo's driverless taxis presented a risk "due to recurring incursions by its vehicles into active construction zones where construction workers, vehicles and equipment are present".

In another incident earlier this month, footage widely seen on social media appeared to show a Waymo car trying to overtake patrol vehicles that were deliberately slowing the pace of traffic on a San Francisco highway.

The California ban, which came into effect on 11 November, would remain in place until Waymo "demonstrates to the department that it has taken effective corrective actions to address the deficiencies that precipitated the restrictions".

A Waymo spokesperson said: "In alignment with our safety-first principles, we have also temporarily restricted freeway operations across our other operating markets to evaluate our performance."

Data on Waymo's website suggests that its cars, compared to human drivers, are involved in 95% fewer crashes resulting in serious injury.

Waymo currently operates driverless taxis in 15 US cities, but only offered freeway rides in a handful of those locations, including San Francisco, Los Angeles and Miami.

The company's driverless taxis can continue to operate on regular city streets.

In May, Waymo recalled thousands of its self-driving cars in the US over a software issue that could allow vehicles to drive into flooded roads.

In the same month, multiple Waymo cars were spotted driving around a cul-de-sac in Atlanta, Georgia, in the early hours of the morning.

#157. Kraken parent's acquisition of Tribe Capital flags new chapter in evolution of crypto exchanges

Payward, the owner of crypto exchange Kraken, is pushing into private markets with its latest acquisition, underscoring a broader shift in cryptocurrencies, from trading-led growth to the buildout of financial infrastructure.

Payward said late Wednesday that it's buying venture capital and asset management provider Tribe Capital Management, led by Payward's co-CEO Arjun Sethi. No purchase price was disclosed.

Tribe, where Sethi is co-founder and chairman, invests across early- and late-stage private companies and boasts $2.7 billion in assets under management.

"Joining Payward gives us the opportunity to bring Tribe's investment approach to a global financial platform," Jonathan Hsu, another co-founder of Tribe Capital, said in a statement. "We see a strong fit between our investment approach and Payward's infrastructure, distribution, and product-building capabilities."

Payward didn't respond to a request for comment.

The acquisition is novel among crypto exchanges. Unlike exchange-owned venture arms such as Coinbase Ventures or Binance Labs, Payward is looking to integrate Tribe's investment and fund-management capabilities into an existing custody, trading, collateral and tokenization infrastructure to develop private-market investment products.

Payward is trying to position itself to capture value from a broader crypto-driven transformation of financial services, not use the venture vehicle to draw more capital into the crypto ecosystem.

The acquisition is another step in the evolution in the crypto exchange business model. With cryptocurrency prices continuing to decline this year, crypto exchanges have worked to move past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, focusing instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance.

#155. Security flaw found in Java, world's third-largest programming language

A tech start-up used AI to uncover a critical software bug in Java that had existed for more than a decade (generic photo)

A cyber-security firm has used artificial intelligence (AI) to uncover a critical flaw in the hugely popular computer programming language Java.

Tech start-up Calif - which recently identified a cyber-security flaw in another language, PHP - found the bug in Java's memory functions.

Java is a popular programming language used by nearly all of the world's top companies such as Apple and Amazon, as well as many banks, hospitals and governments.

It is meant to make the IT world more secure by managing your memory online, said computer scientist Professor Toby Walsh, which is why "this is such an unlikely and serious flaw".

The discovery comes after a string of serious technology issues, including a massive Microsoft outage.

On Tuesday, the US National Security Agency said its internal communications went offline on Tuesday due to a "scheduled routine maintenance".

The NSA did not specify whether its outage was linked to the identified Java bug.

Apple also reported multiple services suffered full outages due to an internal update, while a network failure grounded all flights in the US.

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio said there was "zero evidence" the incidents were related.

With the help of AI, Calif discovered a serious security vulnerability it has called Java4Shell, which allowed an adversary to remotely execute unwanted commands to Java's memory system.

In this case, the adversary was OpenAI's recently-released model GPT-6 Cyber, which is not yet available to the public.

Oracle, the owner of Java, released a "critical patch update" on Tuesday, which it said contained security vulnerability fixes - including for this flaw.

At this stage it is unclear if any platforms or companies have been affected by the latest incident.

But the Java flaw remains a huge concern for cyber-security experts, who say overloading a system's memory is one of the most common ways for hackers to exploit or crash a system.

The BBC has contacted Oracle for comment.

What is PHP and why did it spark a global cyber-security scramble?

US National Security Agency's classified internal network reportedly goes offline

Nearly every critical sector, including banking, national security systems, aviation and telecommunications, relies on Java.

First released in 1995, the programming language and computing system is now the "beating heart of the modern economy", Calif said in its investigation.

One of its main functions is to manage and protect memory for you, Walsh, a leading researcher in AI, told the BBC.

Any time you transfer information on the internet, receive a file from a user or need to log into an IT system with a password, the platform you are using is likely relying on a Java program.

The serious flaw could comprise "critical financial systems, customers and organisation's data," cyber-security expert Dr Pedram Hayati said.

In Australia alone, almost every bank uses Java for managing critical transaction data, Hayati added.

The latest incidents have exposed how nearly every critical sector - including national security systems, aviation and telecommunications - relies on legacy infrastructure online.

Earlier this week, thousands of customers at major US banks, including JP Morgan Chase, Wells Fargo and Bank of America, reported trouble accessing services.

The Java discovery follows a critical cyber-security flaw identified in the PHP programming language, also identified by Calif.

PHP is the code that websites like Wikipedia use to collect information and present it in a way you can read on the web.

Surrey Centre for Cyber Security Prof Alan Woodward said the PHP flaw affected some 30 million websites, leaving them vulnerable to malicious attacks.

At the time, website owners were urged to check if their sites were affected and, if so, they were told to install updates to patch the issue or consider going offline.

The PHP flaws went undetected for at least a decade, which Walsh said is similar to what has happened with Java.

"It's been hiding in plain sight for 12 years", he said.

"AI is now exposing more flaws much more quickly," Walsh said including ones that may have been so obscure and difficult that humans could not have discovered them alone.

Earlier in November, OpenAI president Greg Brockman announced his company was pivoting 25% of its staff to focus on cyber-security demands.

Are we too reliant on big tech companies?

#154. Speed camera fault may have allowed drivers to escape fines

Further problems with the accuracy of speed cameras on English roads have been uncovered which may have allowed drivers to slip through the cracks and escape fines.

National Highways discovered issues with speed cameras last year, affecting 154 out of 400 cameras but, following an investigation, said it had found another fault on units monitoring roads including those running between Huntingdon and Cambridge.

The Home Office has revoked approval that allows footage from the cameras to be used as evidence in legal proceedings. US firm Verra Mobility operates the cameras but National Highways said it will now end the contract.

Roads minister Justin Madders said: "We can't take chances with this.

"If we're going to prosecute people, we need to be confident the system is working as it should. That's why we've taken the action we have."

In a statement, Verra Mobility said it "remains fully committed to delivering reliable, innovative technology that helps make roads safer".

The fault affected all of the variable speed cameras on smart motorways, and a section of the A14 between Huntingdon and Cambridge as well as the A1 approach junction to the A14.

A spokesperson for National Highways said that Verra's camera system had been selected on the basis that it maintained Home Office approval.

The agency said that since that was no longer the case "we are ending our contractual relationship to use the cameras for enforcement".

It added: "We are considering all further commercial and legal options that may be appropriate."

The BBC has contacted Verra Mobility for comment.

Madders said that the enforcement of speed limits continues to be conducted through a range of different tactics, including mobile cameras as well as marked and unmarked police patrols.

But he admitted that the camera system will not be fully fixed until sometime next year.

Rod Dennis, senior policy officer at the RAC motoring organisation, said: "Malfunctioning speed cameras on smart motorways do more than just let speeding drivers get away with breaking the law and endangering lives â they further undermine confidence in the very types of road we know drivers already have serious misgivings about."

There are 4,000 cameras across the entire road network.

In December 2025, National Highways, which runs England's motorways, blamed an "anomaly" in how variable speed cameras were interacting with speed limit signs on some A roads and motorways.

For example, on a road where the speed limit increases, a driver may see a sign showing 60mph but the camera recording it may still be working on the basis of a previous 40mph speed limit.

The fault led to 2,650 wrongful speed camera activations between 2021 and the end of last year.

Those who had been wrongfully fined have been reimbursed.

As National Highways continued to test the system, it discovered a further problem "relating to how some of these cameras may activate in specific and rare circumstances".

It said that according to its calculations, "the circumstances in which the latest issue could occur are extremely rare, and there is no evidence to suggest it has impacted enforcement".

Nevertheless, it said it was "taking immediate steps to ensure compliance is maintained at relevant locations".

It said it would put further traffic officer patrols in place to monitor the sites.

#152. Booking Holdings creates new enterprise division as AI reshapes travel planning

Booking Holdings is bringing together two of its business-to-business travel operations in a new division it hopes will be transformational, betting that a larger, coordinated partner platform can generate growth as artificial intelligence changes how travelers discover and book trips.

The new enterprise unit, dubbed Booking Partner Division, will combine Booking.com Strategic Partnerships with Rocket Travel by Agoda, and is set to begin operating in January. Agoda CEO Omri Morgenshtern will lead the unit, which aims to help businesses such as banks, airlines and online travel companies sell trips or build branded experiences for their customers.

"We realize that if we focus our efforts both on the product side, but also on the go-to-market side, then we can potentially grow faster and serve better partners," Morgenshtern told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

He said existing partners will find new and better products, which will also help grow the partner base.

The move gives Booking a single organization to coordinate technology, travel supply and commercial expertise across business relationships. The company says partners will be able to use its global supply and technology through white-label platforms, integrated applications, branded solutions and hotel-chain rate distribution.

The portfolio includes more than 4.7 million properties, 33 million accommodation listings, more than 500 airlines and more than 200 car rental providers, according to the company.

Competitor Expedia Group has seen notable growth by prioritizing its enterprise efforts, including a strategy launched in 2022 to make its technology platform available to business partners looking to add travel to their own offerings.

From 2018 to 2023, Expedia’s business-to-business revenue grew at a 16% compound annual rate, while adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization, or EBITDA, for the division grew at a 28% rate, the company said in 2024.

In 2025, Expedia’s B2B revenue rose 18% to $4.8 billion, and its segment adjusted EBITDA increased 22% to $1.26 billion. Those figures represented about a third of Expedia’s total revenue and more than a third of its adjusted EBITDA for the full year.

Morgenshtern declined to say how much the new division is expected to contribute to Booking Holdings' overall revenue and profit, but he said the opportunity for growth is immense, estimating the total addressable market at $2 trillion.

Online travel agencies have been pressured this year as investors scrutinize how AI will change the way customers search for and then book their travel.

Companies that offer lodging, experiences and other travel and leisure are grappling with how to stay visible if AI tools become the new starting point for planning and reservations.

Disney CEO Josh D’Amaro said on the company’s earnings call Tuesday that guests already use AI to discover and plan Disney trips, and that some are using the tools to book.

“Ultimately, we want Disney to show up in those tools,” he said, adding that Disney is exploring ways to let guests make reservations directly from them.

Booking anticipates offering partners AI capabilities that draw on the company's existing travel know-how while minimizing token costs, it said.

"I see a lot of AI demos," Morgenshtern said, drawing a distinction between a product that caters to a few customers and one that's scalable and works even when overloaded by travel emergencies. He said he's "super optimistic" about the bespoke product — a vertical agent — that Booking will offer to its partners.

#151. Government backs welfare shake-up to prevent 'lost generation'

The government has backed plans by former minister Alan Milburn for sweeping reforms to prevent "losing a generation" of young people to a life on benefits.

Milburn proposes spending billions on tailored support for under 25s not in employment, education or training (Neets), to be part-funded by cuts to some benefits.

He claims his plan, which also proposes tax breaks for firms and major education reforms, will get 440,000 young people into jobs by 2036.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham said he endorsed the "principles" of the Milburn review and was confident he could persuade Labour MPs to back legislation to implement its measures.

The PM told Sky News he would set out a full response to the review next month, after he had received a separate report into Personal Independence Payments (Pips) by Social Security Minister Sir Stephen Timms.

But he added: "I have never in my years in politics seen a wholesale, whole government approach to supporting the next generation.

"That is what this represents, and I am absolutely confident that I can take this vision and get people behind it, indeed get the country behind it, because we will not place Britain in the position I want it to be in if we have a generation of young people growing up without hope for the future."

The government has made Milburn the head of a new new youth opportunities unit to deliver on his recommendations.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said her party agreed with Milburn's "diagnosis" but claimed Labour had "made the problem much worse for young people" by increasing employers' National Insurance contributions and the youth minimum wage.

There are few signs so far that Burnham will face a backlash from his own MPs of the scale that blocked his predecessor Sir Keir Starmer's planned Â£5bn welfare cuts last year.

But some left-wing Labour MPs and the Unison trade union have warned proposals to cut health-related benefits will drive some young people into poverty.

Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden told MPs that "in future" the welfare state should be judged "not just by a snapshot of people's monthly incomes" but "but by the story of people's lives, and by their ability to achieve their full potential".

McFadden, who was flanked by Burnham and Chancellor John Healey during his statement in the Commons, said the government rejected claims that young people were "snowflakes and shirkers".

The million who were not in work or training had been robbed of the "prospect of a good future" and that was something that demanded "much more" than a conventional government response, he told MPs.

He said the government would publish draft proposals on welfare reform early next year, with legislation to follow.

In his wide-ranging 250 page report, external, Milburn proposes:

An "entirely new benefits system" for under-25s, which would end the health-top up of Universal Credit and Personal Independence Payments (Pip), the main disability benefit in England and Wales

Instead, a new "youth allowance" would be set at the basic rate of Universal Credit for under-25s, with a higher rate for those living independently

A reduction in employers' National Insurance costs for 21- to 24-year-olds, starting in the retail and hospitality sectors, meaning employing those young people would be cheaper

A huge expansion in post-18 college places and a legal right to work, education or training

A new, locally-led Youth Opportunity Service in England to give young people one named advisor and a personalised participation plan

Primary and secondary schools required to identify and support pupils at risk of becoming Neet, and schools obliged to offer work experience

The report calls for the government to spend around Â£5.8bn in total by 2036-37 on extra mental health and employment support.

And it urges ministers to "kickstart the reform programme" next year, with an initial Â£500m, from "repriotising" existing spending.

At a glance: Key proposals from Milburn's Neets report

Why Milburn's Neets reforms will only save Â£200m a year by 2036

Where are the most jobs for 18-24 year-olds?

Milburn has said his plans are not about saving money - and his report says the government will not see overall savings from reduced benefit bills and higher tax receipts until 2036/27, when it will claw back Â£200m.

But by then, it assumes the share of 16â24-year-olds that are Neet will have gone down from 13% - one of the highest in Europe - to 5%, the lowest in the UK since records began.

Launching his report earlier on Tuesday, Milburn said "half measures will not do" because "the scale of the problem is just too big for that".

He added: "Britain is at risk of losing a generation of young people, not because they lack talent, not because they lack ambition, not because they do not want to work.

"This is not a crisis of aspiration; it is a crisis of opportunity."

Milburn's report has no specific blueprint for Scotland, Wales or Northern Ireland, where education, skills, health and local government are devolved.

He said the UK and devolved governments "should agree common definitions, a shared set of headline outcomes and a regular joint review of progress".

Scotland's Education Secretary MÃ iri McAllan said: "Where there is opportunity to collaborate with the UK government and others for the benefit of young people in Scotland, we will take it."

But she argued many of the reforms recommended for England, such as stronger vocational training, were already in place in Scotland.

A spokesperson for the Welsh government said: "We stand ready to work with the UK government to ensure Wales benefits fully from any UK-wide measures that improve life chances through access to rewarding work."

Reform UK leader Nigel Farage said he agreed "with the general drift of the Milburn report, but it's too little and too slow".

"We have a fundamental problem in Britain," he added. "Too many people are being encouraged to go on benefits, and training and apprenticeship schemes that take far too long."

Lib Dem treasury spokesperson Daisy Cooper said if the government wanted to get people into work, it should back high street firms "that give many their first job", scrap the "unfair jobs tax" and negotiate a new growth and defence partnership with the EU.

Green Party leader Zack Polanski described Milburn's proposals as a "flimsy sticking plaster" that intend to "slash support for disabled young people while subsidising corporations to hire cheap labour".

#149. Amazon launches in-app push against Mamdani-backed delivery bill in New York City

Amazon is taking its fight against a New York City delivery bill, which has been backed by Mayor Zohran Mamdani, to its shopping website and app.

Some users who open the company's shopping app or website report being served with pop-ups and banners warning that the New York City Council "could add up to $5 per order" if it passes Intro 518, or the Delivery Protection Act.

The bill would require Amazon to employ its army of third-party delivery companies directly, disrupting the contractor model it has relied on since 2018. Amazon uses a network of thousands of small companies, which often work solely for Amazon, to handle the last-mile portion of shipments, in an effort to speed up deliveries and control more of its logistics operations.

"If this legislation passes, we'd no longer be able to work with them, and we'd be forced to consider moving our operations outside of the city," Amazon's in-app message states. "That would likely mean slower and more expensive delivery for millions of customers in New York City, while also threatening small businesses and their thousands of employees."

The proposed legislation was introduced in February and is awaiting review by City Council Speaker Julie Menin.

Amazon also posted a statement on its company website where it often publishes its policy positions. But rarely has the company used its valuable e-commerce site and app real estate to push its perspectives. Other tech companies including Uber and Lyft have similarly used their apps to advocate for or against legislation in the past.

Amazon's pop-up notices and a stationary banner on its store homepage direct users to a form where they can "contact your council member to oppose Intro 518."

According to Amazon, 20,000 customers sent emails to city council members in the first six hours that the pop-up appeared.

Menin told CNBC in a statement that Amazon's claim it will need to raise prices due to the proposed legislation "defies logic."

"We are crafting legislation that regulates Amazon and protects workers, without impacting prices or jobs," Menin said. "If Amazon thinks this deceptive campaign will effectively threaten New Yorkers and intimidate this City Council, they are dead wrong."

Amazon spokesperson Kelly Nantel said in a statement that the company put up the notices to be "transparent with our customers" about what the bill "would cause them if it passes as written."

"If customers are concerned, one of the most impactful things they can do is contact their City Council member and ask them to oppose this bill," Nantel said.

The Delivery Protection Act has received a swell of support from labor groups, including the International Brotherhood of Teamsters, which has sought to unionize Amazon delivery and warehouse workers.

Mamdani endorsed the bill in August, saying Amazon's subcontractor model "leaves workers vulnerable and corporations free to avoid accountability for reckless conditions on city streets." He's argued the bill would lead to safer working conditions for drivers, and allow the city to "regulate last-mile operations."

Amazon disputed Mamdani's claims, saying the bill would put Delivery Service Partner jobs "at risk without improving safety." It also argued that other businesses and governments use contractors in their operations, and said the DSP model has allowed it to meet growing customer demand.

"It works because local entrepreneurs — people rooted in their communities — deliver a better experience," Amazon said. "They invest in their teams, their routes, and their neighborhoods. That's not a liability play."

Amazon has lobbied heavily against the bill, while logistics and business groups have launched local advertising campaigns, and warned they may sue the city if the bill passes.

#147. Jeff Bezos says he was 'heavily involved' in casting Robert Pattinson as new Bond

Watch: Jeff Bezos on involvement in casting Robert Pattinson as James Bond

Jeff Bezos has said he was "heavily involved" in casting Robert Pattinson as the new James Bond.

Speaking at an event in California, the Amazon chairman said he was originally "biased against" Pattinson - who starred in many blockbuster films like Batman and the Twilight series - "because he's already so famous".

The role of 007 has typically gone to an actor who is not as well known at the time of casting. But Bezos said in screen tests, "it was so obvious he is James Bond".

This will be the first 007 film in the official series not under the creative control of the Broccoli family, who had overseen the franchise since the first movie in 1962. Amazon purchased MGM, which co-owns the franchise, in 2022.

Robert Pattinson: How the Twilight star became 007

'Good Bond vibes' - Hollywood locals react to Robert Pattinson cast as new 007

"There was something special about Rob", Bezos said at Semafor Silicon Valley & The World Event on Tuesday.

Pattinson was unveiled as the franchise's seventh James Bond on Monday, taking over from actor Daniel Craig. He will star in the 26th Bond movie, due to be released in 2028.

In a statement, the London-born actor said: "James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honour of a lifetime."

The next Bond film will be directed by Denis Villeneuve, but its title and exact release date have not yet been announced.

The announcement comes after years of intense speculation about who will take over from Craig in one of the most high-profile - and highly scrutinised - roles in entertainment.

Robert Pattinson was unveiled as the franchise's seventh James Bond on Monday

Several other actors, including Josh O'Connor, Callum Turner and Jack Lowden, were reported to be in the frame for the role as screen tests took place in recent weeks.

But Amazon MGM ultimately went with a household name, choosing a more bankable movie star who is widely seen as a safe pair of hands.

"We ran a really tight process and made the final decision on Friday," Bezos said.

There were five finalists who went through "real, old-fashioned screen tests", acting out three different scenes in full costume, with real sets and other actors, he said.

"By the time we got to this list of five, they were all amazing. But there was something special about Rob and his performance," Bezos added. "It was immediately un-debated. Denis [Villeneuve], the director â¦ we all gravitated toward Rob."

The pick comes amid widespread Hollywood cuts and consolidation, with studios struggling to get people into cinemas.

Hollywood insiders have said Pattinson was viewed as a safe pick to bring audiences in, rather than taking a risk on a new actor who could grow with the franchise.

"Maybe he's boring. Maybe Hollywood would be passing up a chance to anoint a badly needed new star. But with so much at stake for everyone involved, that all makes him the obvious pick," Hollywood journalist Matthew Belloni, who predicted the pick, wrote in his newsletter.

The BBC's Regan Morris contributed to this report.

#145. How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Nvidia and its co-founder Jensen Huang have been at the centre of the artificial intelligence (AI) spending boom.

No company or chief executive has arguably been more consequential to the development of AI in recent years, with the US microchip manufacturer under Huang positioning itself as a critical cog in the development of the world-changing technology.

That partnership has now ended following Huang's resignation over revelations of extramarital affairs, one of which is alleged to have been with a Chinese spy.

The company has been rocked by the scandal, with hundreds of billions of dollars wiped off its stock market value and questions swirling about the extent of the impact of Huang's departure.

While Nvidia is not as much of a household name as other Big Tech players such as Apple, Amazon and Microsoft, the company has been just as integral to the booming US stock market of recent years.

The billions of dollars being funnelled into AI and the construction of data centres has bolstered the American economy, with Nvidia being one of the biggest spenders.

Originally focusing on chips which process graphics for computer games, it pivoted to making the chips used in AI systems and now dominates the market.

It is now one the most valuable firms, worth $3.8 trillion (Â£2.9 trillion), and on Tuesday said its rapid growth is poised to continue as the firm's quarterly sales doubled from a year earlier.

But there's now an air of uncertainty over Nvidia's future direction. Huang played a major role in the company, leading its shift towards AI, and maintained a firm grip on the company, with up to 200 people directly reporting to him.

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at investment firm AJ Bell, says because Huang was also a key figure in "flying the flag" to drum up enthusiasm and investment for the wider AI industry, his departure posed a "real risk" that Nvidia could now decide to change its current strategy and rein in spending.

Such a move could spell a wider slowdown in AI investment, which would have an economic impact. How big? That's "unknown", Coatsworth says.

"If they decide to go with the much more cautious route... there could be a knock-on effect in that you have got various companies in the AI infrastructure space suddenly finding they don't have a key source of finance," he says.

Kunjan Sobhani, senior technology industry analyst at Bloomberg Intelligence, expects the impact of Huang leaving to be "limited" for now as Nvidia has strong commitments and a deep order book. But he said this picture could change in the long run.

That is because Nvidia, on its journey to the top of the AI industry, has created a spider's web of deals through what is known as "circular financing".

Not only does it sell microchips to its customers, which include the likes of Meta, SpaceX and OpenAI, it has also provided funds for them and other firms in the supply chain by either handing out loans or investing in them directly.

The practice has prompted concerns among some investors, who worry what might happen if Nvidia were to turn off the spending taps. If investment dried up, would other firms in the supply chain cut spending or jobs themselves? Would that spread to the wider US economy?

That risk is perhaps why tech bosses including Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, the CEO of Fortnite maker Epic Games, have called for Huang's reinstatement.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation, which has also prompted a campaign by shareholders and supporters to "Bring Jensen Back".

Huang himself has said he is open to a return, just days after resigning.

Coatsworth says he has never seen circular financing used at this scale before, but admits there are competitive rivals around, which could insulate the wider AI industry from any potential slowdown at the firm.

When Nvidia's share price plunged in the wake of the affair revelations, the stock of its rival semiconductor makers including AMD, Broadcom and Marvell Technology jumped.

"I don't think all AI activity grinds to a halt if Nvidia slows down what it's doing and if it pulls some financing, but it does change how people would look at the whole situation," Coatsworth adds.

The risk of a slowdown has already rattled stock markets. Investors took fright at Democrat gains in the midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will actually pay off.

Investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, who said the technology would remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

Nvidia's interim boss Colette Kress said it was "business as usual" without Huang at the helm as the company's rapid growth remains on track.

The value of the shares in Big Tech companies have cushioned the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs, buoying investments and pensions for people worldwide.

Despite Huang saying he would be "honoured" to return, Nvidia's board has said it is not considering bringing him back.

And while Coatsworth thinks "no CEO is irreplaceable", he admits the pressure is on for whoever succeeds him.

#142. Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

Jensen Huang and Nvidia's interim CEO, Colette Kress

Nvidia has firmly ruled out letting ousted co-founder Jensen Huang return as it revealed that sales doubled to more than $100bn.

In the company's first results since Huang's dramatic exit last week, covering the three month period just before the recent scandal surfaced, interim chief executive Colette Kress said it was "business as usual". She told investors: "We've got this."

Earlier on Tuesday, Huang said he would be "honoured" to return and lead Nvidia, having quit following alleged extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected spy for China.

But Nvidia's chair Harvey Jones said: "The board does not intend to reinstate him."

Nvidia reported that sales for the three months to 25 October rose to $115.8bn (Â£86.9bn), with shares in the company jumping by 4.8% in after-hours trading.

The company's profits also doubled from a year ago rising to $65bn. Sales from data centres, a significant part of Nvidia's business, also doubled to $107bn.

The company said its rapid growth is poised to continue and revenue will rise by another 13% in the next quarter.

How important are Nvidia and Jensen Huang to the US economy?

Given the uncertainty surrounding the company after Huang's exit, financial analysts had been unsure that Nvidia would provide any forward-looking guidance.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior analyst for trading platform Capital.com, said Nvidia's results "offer a strong answer to concerns that demand for AI infrastructure is cooling" and temper immediate worries that Huang was irreplaceable as a leader.

"The harder question is whether Nvidia can preserve the strategic advantage Huang helped build," Hathorn added. "His exit is unlikely to change orders already committed, but could matter more when Nvidia makes its next round of decisions on products, partnerships and investment."

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

Kress seemed eager to stress that Nvidia can continue to not only succeed, but lead the ongoing AI buildout that is happening throughout the technology industry.

"It is business as usual," she said. "Our partners, our suppliers, all of them are 100% behind us."

She added that Nvidia's leadership team has "stood up very strongly over the last week... working seamlessly in all of their departments and projects".

"Our momentum is real. Our demand is accelerating. Our supply is growing. Our customers and our partners are with us, and employees are committed." Kress said before leaving the analyst call. "We've got this."

The interim chief executive also said Nvidia is no longer shipping Hopper data centre semiconductors to China.

The Hopper technology is in Nvidia's advanced H200 processors, which the firm won approval to sell in China after Huang's repeated lobbying efforts in Washington.

They had previously been restricted over concerns that selling them to China would give its industries and military an edge over the US.

Kress did not elaborate on why the shipments had been stopped.

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

Jones saying earlier that the company would not answer analysts' questions about Huang or Nvidia's investigation into his conduct, he did address the search for a permanent replacement.

He said Nvidia is working with an executive search firm and is considering internal and external candidates, but that the company would not rush its selection.

"With the advanced technology and the national security dynamics in the marketplace, our new chief executive will have to navigate those waters carefully," Jones said. "The search will be thorough and we want to do it as quickly as possible, but we won't do it before we find the exact right person."

Michael Monaghan from investment firm Founder ETFs is among those who want Huang back in the top job at Nvidia.

The Nvidia investor told the BBC that he is waiting to see the results of a thorough investigation into Huang's actions, instead of relying on "salacious allegations" made in the media.

Nvidia's strong earnings are "from the machine that Jensen built" and a company tends to lose "its shine and vision" after an iconic founder leaves, Monaghan said.

#141. Heating bills, chocolate and cat food: What the inflation figures mean for you

Nobody will be celebrating the fact that prices in the UK are rising at the fastest pace for a year.

Add to that an expectation of higher household energy bills and sharpening price increases and things look grim for everyone.

But there are some signs of hope that may take the edge off the rising cost of living. Here's how.

That headline is not missing a decimal point. The latest prediction for the increase in domestic energy prices in the new year is, indeed, 26%.

Millions of homes in England, Scotland and Wales are on tariffs affected by regulator Ofgem's price cap, which is set every three months. The next change for January will be officially announced next Wednesday, 25 November.

Energy consultancy Cornwall Insight, which has a good track record with its predictions, suggests that a household using a typical amount of gas and electricity will pay Â£2,166 a year - that's an increase of Â£443 from current levels.

Not all energy use for the year is between January and March, but a big chunk is owing to the cold, dark days and nights.

How much you pay depends on your use, with heavy users of gas likely to be the hardest hit.

The government has promised a Â£100 payment for people living in homes in council tax bands A to D in England to help cover winter energy costs. Other nations are planning similar support.

The latest inflation data shows that government assistance can have an impact on the general rate of inflation, external, as recorded by the Office for National Statistics (ONS).

Domestic energy prices rose by nearly 4% in October, but this was offset in inflationary terms by a cut in VAT on electricity bills.

While the rate of inflation in general was 3.6% in the year to October, food prices only went up by 0.9%.

It is partly the result of intense competition between the supermarkets. Like many retailers, they are facing higher costs but are not passing that onto their customers in full.

There may be some scars from the recent cost-of-living spike here. Shoppers suffered a period during the big rise in inflation in 2022-23 when food prices rocketed.

Those higher prices are embedded. While food prices overall aren't coming down now, they aren't rising anywhere near as fast as back then.

The detail here is very interesting. Take chocolate: world cocoa prices are down since 2024, and now chocolate prices are falling - down 2.9%, according to the latest data.

But the ONS has also pointed to cat food as one, albeit small, driver of the faster rate of rising prices.

Should high energy and fuel costs - which are driving the rising rate of inflation - persist, there are questions over whether the cost of producing and transporting food also goes up, and the chances of that higher cost being passed on to consumers are greater.

Economists have been closely studying whether there are any signs of those so-called second-round effects - where an initial price shock spreads to wages, services and broader consumer prices. So far, there are relatively few.

If prices rise then it takes a bigger chunk of people's income and so has a deeper impact on those whose finances are already stretched, so the latest data on food prices is good news for many people.

Typical energy bill forecast to go up by Â£443 a year from January as inflation rate rises again

One set of economists who will consider whether there is evidence of second-round effects are the nine people who set interest rates, which heavily influences the cost of borrowing money - such as mortgages - as well as the returns people get on savings.

The Bank of England's Monetary Policy Committee meets again in December and, after that, in February.

Having just raised interest rates - for the first time in more than three years - to 4%, there's a widespread expectation among analysts that another hike is to come.

To be clear, we don't know if and when that will happen for sure.

The case for an increase would be if prices continue to accelerate. The Bank will want to slow that down.

The case against would be price rises remaining relatively confined to energy-related costs. Any easing of Middle East tensions could limit price rises without Bank of England intervention.

As far as wages are concerned, the latest official data suggests that rising prices are largely covered by rising pay.

That is an average - so is not true for everyone - but how long it remains the case may be key to how deep upcoming price rises cut into our personal finances.

How is the rising cost of living affecting your quality of life?

#138. Lowe's posts strong online sales, raises outlook despite slow housing market

Lowe's on Wednesday posted strong quarterly results and raised its full-year outlook as the company said it saw strength among professional shoppers.

The company said comparable sales grew 1.8% for the fiscal third quarter, driven by a 17.4% increase in online sales and "solid" performance in pro and home services sales. Net sales rose to $22.76 billion.

Because of the strength, Lowe's now said it expects full-year sales of roughly $92.5 billion, up from a previous projection of $92 billion. It also expects comparable sales to be up 0.5%, compared with previous guidance of flat.

CEO Marvin Ellison told CNBC that the company is focused on growing despite economic headwinds and a sluggish housing market. He said Lowe's posted its sixth straight quarterly comparable sales increase and saw particular strength in appliances, which saw its largest revenue growth in five years.

"We believe irrespective of the macro environment we're in, we're going to take share because there's a lot of share out there that we can go and get, and we're going to be aggressive to go and get that share," Ellison told CNBC.

Lowe's also reported a pretax benefit of approximately $250 million from tariff refunds during the quarter, which contributed a 34-cent boost to earnings per share. Ellison said the company plans to use those funds to enhance the customer experience and bring value to shoppers.

He added that do-it-yourself customers and pro shoppers are not investing in major renovation projects but are instead spending on repair and maintenance projects.

"We are giving those pros what they need," he said. "We're able to improve our fulfillment capabilities and our pro loyalty program."

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter ended Oct. 30, Lowe's reported net income of $1.67 billion, or $2.98 per share, compared with $1.62 billion, or $2.88 per share, the year prior.

The home improvement retailer has previously said it's seeing "pressure" on spending on projects, especially as consumers are stretched thin by inflation and macroeconomic uncertainty. The company is also battling a slower housing market and higher mortgage rates.

Ellison previously told CNBC he expects consumers to remain cautious through the end of the year.

Lowe's executives said on a call with analysts Wednesday that the company is continuing to battle high fuel costs, which they said accelerated through the third quarter. CFO Brandon Sink said diesel prices are currently around $6 per gallon, up about 15% overall compared to what the company saw in the second quarter.

"So we're trying to continue to manage that, balance that, and we're reflecting that into our Q4 guide," Sink said.

Ellison told CNBC the company remains optimistic about consumer spending this holiday season and that November sales are in line with expectations so far.

Lowe's competitor Home Depot said earlier this week that it's seeing resilience among professional shoppers and reported comparable sales growth.
